B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

报告第二例 麻疹 病例后,官员们致力于阻止疾病传播 - 今天奥斯威戈县

Officials Aim to Stop Disease Spread as Second Measles Case Reported - Oswego County Today
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 8 日,奥斯威戈县卫生部门确认了第二例 麻疹 病例,患者为一名未接种疫苗的成年居民,目前正在康复中,不再具有传染性。第一例病例也是在未接种疫苗的个体中报告的,两者之间存在关联。卫生部门强调,由于病例之间存在关联,公众面临的风险仍然很低,并正在提供有关 麻疹 症状和预防的指导。他们在 Nick Sterio 公共卫生诊所每周提供 MMR 疫苗 诊所,以帮助防止进一步传播。官员们强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,并指出两种剂量对 麻疹 传播 的有效率为 97%。为了保护隐私,没有透露更多患者详细信息。除非存在暴露风险,该部门近期不会发布新增确诊病例的新闻稿。鼓励居民检查其 疫苗接种 状态并联系卫生部门进行预约或获取信息。

时间

地点与对象
纽约州奥斯威戈县。
事件
奥斯威戈县卫生部门宣布另一例 麻疹 确诊病例,患者为一名未接种疫苗的成年居民。第二个人与第一例确诊病例住在一起,目前正在康复,不再传染给其他人。
规模、影响与风险
目前有 1 例 麻疹 确诊病例,尚未发布更多患者信息。由于两起案件之间的联系,公众面临的风险仍然很低。麻疹病毒 是一种高度传染性呼吸道疾病,可能导致严重的健康并发症,特别是对于 5 岁以下的儿童。
应对、状态与不确定性
奥斯威戈县卫生部门正在密切监测情况,并提供有关 麻疹 症状和预防策略的持续指导。他们每周提供 麻疹、腮腺炎和风疹 (MMR) 疫苗 免疫诊所。由于对公众的风险有限,近期不会发布新确诊的 麻疹 病例的新闻稿。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Officials Aim to Stop Disease Spread as Second Measles Case Reported - Oswego County Today

News Brief
On October 8, 2026, the Oswego County Health Department confirmed a second case of measles in an unvaccinated adult resident, who is now recovering and no longer contagious. The first case was also reported in an unvaccinated individual, and both are connected. The health department emphasized that the risk to the public remains low, as the cases are linked, and is providing guidance on measles symptoms and prevention. They are offering weekly MMR vaccine clinics at the Nick Sterio Public Health Clinic to help prevent further spread. Officials stressed the importance of vaccination, noting that two doses are 97% effective against measles transmission. No additional patient details were released to protect privacy. The department will not issue a news release for new confirmed cases in the near future unless there is a risk of exposure. Residents are encouraged to check their vaccination status and contact the health department for appointments or information.

Emergency Posts Officials Aim to Stop Disease Spread as Second Measles Case Reported Public Risk Remains Low – October 8, 2026 Contributor Discover more Community Announcements Service Letters to Editor Submission Local Business Directory OSWEGO COUNTY – The Oswego County Health Department announced another confirmed case of measles in an unvaccinated adult resident. This second individual resides with the first confirmed case and is now recovering and no longer contagious to others. No additional patient information will be released to protect their privacy. “Our County Health Department is doing a great job monitoring this situation closely,” said Oswego County Legislator Paul House, District 8, chairman of the Legislature’s Health Committee. “They have been providing ongoing guidance about measles symptoms and prevention strategies to the public, and especially to anyone who may have been exposed to the virus, to help reduce any further spread of the disease.” Dr. Christina Liepke, Oswego County Medical Director, said the risk to the public remains low right now because of how these two cases are connected. “Still, the measles virus is a highly contagious respiratory disease that is easily spread by coughing and sneezing,” she said. “I want to remind everyone to be aware of its symptoms, which include a rash with a high fever and cough. Also, please call your health care provider before going into their office to avoid spreading it to other patients and medical staff.” Oswego County Public Health Director Vera Dunsmoor also stressed the importance of vaccination in preventing the spread of disease. “Vaccines not only help protect children and adults from preventable diseases but also help build ‘herd immunity’ to safeguard communities from potential outbreaks,” she said. “Please check your vaccination status and make sure your kids are up to date with their vaccinations as well.” For optimal protection, the federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine, with the first dose given to children aged 12 to 15 months, and the second dose given between 4 to 6 years of age. “Once again, I urge residents to get vaccinated,” she stressed. “It’s the best way to protect yourselves and your children. Just two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is 97% effective against disease transmission – for a lifetime! The Oswego County Health Department offers weekly immunization clinics every Tuesday at the Nick Sterio Public Health Clinic, 70 Bunner St., Oswego. Adults and children can receive various vaccinations by appointment only from 9 to 11 a.m. and 1 to 3 p.m. Most insurances are accepted and no one will be turned away. To make a vaccination appointment, call the health department’s Preventive Division at 315-349-3547. The measles virus is a highly contagious respiratory disease that is easily spread by coughing and sneezing. Initial symptoms include a high fever, cough, red eyes and runny nose. One to four days later, a rash develops on the face and spreads to the rest of the body, hands and feet. A person with measles is considered contagious from four days before the rash appears to four days afterward. While the incubation period for measles is typically 7-14 days, symptoms can appear up to 21 days after exposure. Anyone not protected from it is at risk of serious health complications, especially those under 5 years of age. Measles during pregnancy can increase the risk of early labor, miscarriage and low-birth-weight infants. To make a vaccination appointment, report a potential exposure, or learn more about the measles virus and vaccine, call your primary care provider or the health department’s Preventive Division at 315-349-3547. Due to the limited risk to the public at this time, the Oswego County Health Department will not issue a news release for new confirmed measles cases in the immediate future. If and when it is determined there is a risk for potential exposure, the public will be notified immediately. For up-to-date reports on confirmed measles cases, click on New York State Department of Health’s (NYSDOH’s) measles dashboard. It is updated weekly, on Tuesday afternoons, and shows cases reported through Saturday of the previous week. For more general information about the measles virus, click on NYSDOH. The Legislature’s Health Committee oversees the Health Department. In addition to Legislator House, committee members include Vice Chairman Roy E. Reehil, District 5; Gena Bartlett, District 24; Dorothy Caldwell, District 17; Richard Kline, District 12; Michael Solowy, District 23; and Lee B. Walker, Jr., District 15. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases surpass 1,000 in Pennsylvania amid the state's largest outbreak in decades - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
Health officials in Pennsylvania have reported 74 new measles cases, marking the state's largest outbreak in decades. The outbreak, confirmed by official health reports, has raised concerns about public health impacts. While the exact scale of the outbreak and specific risk levels are not detailed in the evidence, the increase in cases suggests a significant challenge for local health authorities. The situation is currently under review, with no explicit mention of testing, isolation, or contact tracing measures being implemented. The outbreak highlights the importance of vaccination efforts and public health preparedness in the face of such challenges. Further details and actions are expected as the investigation continues.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

宾夕法尼亚州面临数十年来最严重的 麻疹 暴发,病例数增至 1,000 例 - 《卫报》

Pennsylvania faces largest measles outbreak in decades as case count rises to 1,000 - The Guardian
新闻简报据州卫生官员称,宾夕法尼亚州正在经历数十年来最大规模的 麻疹 麻疹,截至 2026 年 10 月 1 日,已有 1,004 例确诊病例。暴发 于 2026 年 4 月开始,已导致 198 人住院治疗,5 人死亡,主要影响兰开斯特县,该县是大量阿米什人和门诺派人口的居住地。纽约州和俄亥俄州等邻近州也报告了重大病例,纽约州宣布进入紧急状态。CDC 报告全国确诊病例 3,887 例,其中宾夕法尼亚州确诊病例最多。然而,由于分类存在争议,该州报告的死亡人数并未包含在疾病预防控制中心的统计中。州长乔什·夏皮罗 (Josh Shapiro) 扩大了 MMR 疫苗 在受影响地区的访问范围,强调需要 疫苗接种 来防止进一步传播。暴发 强调了 疫苗接种 利率下降所带来的风险,该利率已降至防止 暴发 所需的 95% 阈值以下。CDC 也在监测局势,计划于 11 月召开会议,评估美国和墨西哥的 麻疹 无状态。
查看新闻五要素
时间
宾夕法尼亚州的 暴发 于 2026 年 4 月开始,过去三个月病例数不断增加。截至 2026 年 10 月 1 日,CDC 报告了 3,887 例确诊病例,暴发 正在进行中。
地点与对象
暴发 以宾夕法尼亚州为中心,特别是兰开斯特县,该县有大量阿米什人和门诺派人口。纽约州和俄亥俄州等邻近州也受到影响。
事件
截至 2026 年 10 月 1 日,宾夕法尼亚州卫生官员确认了 1,004 例 麻疹 病例,这是三十多年来该州最大的 麻疹 病例。暴发 归因于 疫苗接种 发病率低,已导致 198 人住院治疗,5 人死亡。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 10 月 1 日,CDC 报告全国有 3,887 例 麻疹 确诊病例,其中宾夕法尼亚州病例数最多。宾夕法尼亚州已有 5 人死亡、198 人住院治疗。由于 疫苗接种 比率较低且可能传播到其他州,暴发 被认为是重大公共卫生风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
宾夕法尼亚州卫生部门已扩大受影响社区对 MMR 疫苗 的使用范围。然而,由于州官员与 CDC 之间的争议,与 麻疹 相关的 死亡率 的分类仍然存在不确定性。疾病预防控制中心目前列出了今年美国两例 麻疹 死亡病例,但宾夕法尼亚州报告的死亡人数并未包含在统计中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania faces largest measles outbreak in decades as case count rises to 1,000 - The Guardian

News Brief
Pennsylvania is experiencing its largest measles outbreak in decades, with 1,004 confirmed cases as of 1 October 2026, according to state health officials. The outbreak, which began in April 2026, has led to 198 hospitalizations and five deaths, primarily affecting Lancaster County, home to large Amish and Mennonite populations. Neighboring states like New York and Ohio are also reporting significant cases, with New York declaring a state of emergency. The CDC reported 3,887 confirmed cases nationwide, with the highest numbers in Pennsylvania. However, the state’s reported deaths are not included in the CDC’s count due to a dispute over classification. Governor Josh Shapiro has expanded MMR vaccine access in affected areas, emphasizing the need for vaccination to prevent further spread. The outbreak highlights the risk posed by declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold needed to prevent outbreaks. The CDC is also monitoring the situation, with a meeting planned in November to assess the US and Mexico’s measles-free status.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: efired/Getty Images View image in fullscreen The US is enduring its worst year for measles since 1991. Photograph: efired/Getty Images Pennsylvania faces largest measles outbreak in decades as case count rises to 1,000 Neighboring states are also reporting hundreds of cases, with over 3,800 recorded across the country Pennsylvania has surpassed 1,000 measles cases this year, state health officials calculate, making it the largest state outbreak in more than three decades. The benchmark comes as neighboring New York declared a state of emergency about measles rates there. Pennsylvania’s health department said on Monday 1,004 cases of measles had been confirmed. Five Pennsylvania residents have died after contracting the disease, with 198 hospitalizations. Lancaster county, with sizable Amish and Mennonite populations, remains the hardest hit, with 391 cases, according to official figures. Cases numbers have risen over the past three months, with the highest number of infections in the 24-49 age range. Pennsylvania health officials brace for expected spread of measles outbreak Read more The numbers, however, are not included in a CDC count due to an ongoing dispute between Pennsylvania’s governor, Josh Shapiro, and the US health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, over how to classify measles-related mortality. Shapiro has called Kennedy “incompetent”, and Kennedy has publicly questioned whether the state’s recorded fatalities were directly caused by measles. The CDC – which Kennedy oversees – currently lists two measles deaths in the US this year. Shapiro has expanded access to the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine in affected communities. “I want every Pennsylvanian to know this: measles does not care about your county, your faith, or your social circle,” he said in August. “I urge everyone to take this seriously and to discuss any concerns with their trusted health care provider.” In New York state, Governor Kathy Hochul declared a state of emergency on Monday as well, citing 108 cases in the state as of 3 October. Ohio, which also shares a border with Pennsylvania, has also reported advancing numbers of cases, with more than 200 this year. Like in Pennsylvania, Ohio has a significant Amish population. The US is enduring its worst year for measles since 1991. As of 1 October, the CDC reported 3,887 confirmed cases this year, along with the highest number of measles-associated deaths in decades. In addition to Pennsylvania, outbreaks have been reported in several states, including Texas and Utah, as well as South Carolina, which had the largest outbreak with 997 cases reported. Health officials in Pennsylvania have said the state’s current outbreak started in April, west of Philadelphia and attributed the outbreak to low vaccination rates. According to the Associated Press, the most recent outbreak to surpass 1,000 cases occurred in New York City in the early 1990s. That outbreak infected more than 4,500 people. During the 1989 and 1991 resurgences, 55,000 infections and 123 deaths ⁠were recorded, according to Reuters. The domestic US spread of measles was eliminated in 2000 by high vaccination rates, but those rates have fallen in recent years to below the 95% coverage needed to prevent outbreaks. In November, international health officials will meet to determine if the US and Mexico have lost their measles-free status. The outbreak in Pennsylvania started in April 2026, with cases rising over the past three months. The CDC reported 3,887 confirmed cases as of 1 October 2026, and the outbreak is ongoing.
Location and population
The outbreak is centered in Pennsylvania, particularly in Lancaster County, which has a significant Amish and Mennonite population. Neighboring states like New York and Ohio are also affected.
Event
Pennsylvania health officials confirmed 1,004 measles cases as of 1 October 2026, marking the largest state outbreak in over three decades. The outbreak is attributed to low vaccination rates and has led to 198 hospitalizations and five deaths.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 1 October 2026, the CDC reported 3,887 confirmed measles cases nationwide, with the highest number of cases in Pennsylvania. Five deaths and 198 hospitalizations have been recorded in Pennsylvania. The outbreak is considered a significant public health risk due to low vaccination rates and potential spread to other states.
Response, status and uncertainty
Pennsylvania’s health department has expanded access to the MMR vaccine in affected communities. However, there is ongoing uncertainty regarding the classification of measles-related mortality due to a dispute between state officials and the CDC. The CDC currently lists two measles deaths in the US this year, but Pennsylvania’s reported fatalities are not included in their count.

宾夕法尼亚州 麻疹 暴发 病例数突破 1,000 例,成为美国 3 年来最大的病例 - 雅虎新闻

Pennsylvania Measles Outbreak Tops 1,000 Cases, Becoming the Largest in the U.S. in 3 Decades - Yahoo News Canada
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 暴发 病例总数已达到 1,004 例,成为美国 30 年来最大的病例。暴发 于 2026 年 4 月开始,已导致 198 人住院治疗,5 人死亡。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部报告了这些数据,传染病专家警告 暴发 可能还会持续几个月。CDC 指出,今年美国已报告 3,887 例 麻疹 病例,其中新增 42 例 麻疹 病例。卫生官员强调,低 疫苗接种 率是病毒传播的关键因素,因为只有不到 1% 的确诊病例完全接种了疫苗。疾病预防控制中心表示,如果宾夕法尼亚州提出正式请求,它准备协助宾夕法尼亚州,但尚未收到正式请求。暴发 引起了人们对儿童脆弱性的担忧,他们患肝炎、脑部炎症和继发感染等并发症的风险较高。
查看新闻五要素
时间
宾夕法尼亚州卫生部于 10 月星期一报告了 麻疹 暴发 更新。麻疹 于 2026 年 4 月开始,截至 2026 年 10 月 5 日,已记录 1,004 例病例。仅上周就报告了 106 例病例。
地点与对象
暴发 正在美国宾夕法尼亚州发生。受影响人群包括各个年龄段的个人,其中32%的确诊病例年龄在18岁以下。卫生部门指出,只有不到 1% 的确诊者完全接种了疫苗。
事件
宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 麻疹 病例总数已达到 1,004 例,成为美国最大的病例。麻疹 已导致 198 人住院治疗,5 人死亡。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部报告了这些数据,传染病专家警告说,暴发 可能还会持续几个月。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 1 月 5 日,宾夕法尼亚州记录了 1,004 起 麻疹 病例,超过了南卡罗来纳州的 麻疹。健康专家表示,较低的 麻疹 发病率导致了病毒的传播,CDC 指出,今年美国已报告 3,887 例 麻疹 病例,其中新增 42 例 麻疹 病例。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania Measles Outbreak Tops 1,000 Cases, Becoming the Largest in the U.S. in 3 Decades - Yahoo News Canada

News Brief
A measles outbreak in Pennsylvania has reached 1,004 total cases, making it the largest in the U.S. in 30 years. The outbreak began in April 2026 and has led to 198 hospitalizations and five deaths. The Pennsylvania Department of Health reported these figures, and infectious disease experts warn the outbreak may continue for several more months. The CDC has noted that 3,887 measles cases have been reported in the U.S. this year, with 42 new outbreaks. Health officials emphasize that low vaccination rates are a key factor in the spread of the virus, as fewer than 1% of diagnosed cases were fully vaccinated. The CDC has stated it is prepared to assist Pennsylvania if a formal request is made, but has not yet received one. The outbreak has raised concerns about the vulnerability of children, who are at higher risk of complications such as hepatitis, brain inflammation, and secondary infections.
Original source text (English)
NEED TO KNOW Pennsylvania's Department of Health said in an update on Monday, Oct. 5 that it recorded 1,004 total cases of measles Nearly 200 people have been hospitalized and five deaths have been reported since the outbreak began in April An infectious disease expert said he expects cases to continue for at least another three to six months Pennsylvania has recorded 1,000 measles cases, making it the largest outbreak in the U.S. in three decades. Pennsylvania's Department of Health shared in an update on Monday, Oct. 5 that it logged 1,004 total cases of measles in 2026, surpassing the number of South Carolina's measles outbreak earlier this year, according to Associated Press , NBC News and ABC News . The current outbreak started in April, and since then, there have been 992 measles cases recorded. Last week alone, 106 cases were reported, according to the health department's data. So far, 198 people have been hospitalized and five people have died. Stock photo of a blood sample with measles virus. Credit: Getty According to the health department, fewer than 1% of people diagnosed with measles were fully vaccinated. Health experts said low vaccination rates contributing to the outbreaks, per the AP. Dr. John Goldman, an infectious disease expert at the University of Pittsburgh Medical Center, told NBC News that he expects the measles outbreak in Pennsylvania to continue on for several more months. "I would bet that this goes on for at least another three to six months," he told the outlet. "We have seen an increase in vaccinations, but we aren't seeing enough people get vaccinated to stop the epidemic." The Pennsylvania Department of Health also reported that 32% of those diagnosed with measles were under 18, and 18% of those pediatric patients required hospitalization. Dr. Evan Shirey, a pediatric infectious disease physician at Penn State Health Golisano Children's Hospital, told NBC News that children are particularly vulnerable to measles and its complications. "One of the more common things that the measles virus can do is cause hepatitis or infection of the liver," she told the outlet. "These children will feel very sick, very nauseous, with abdominal pain and vomiting, and they can't take a bottle or can't keep food or liquid down." She said measles can also cause brain inflammation in children, potentially resulting in irreversible neurological damage or secondary bacterial infections. Stock photo of a doctor with a patient after a vaccination. Credit: iStockphoto/Getty Pennsylvania isn't the only state that has battled a measles outbreak this year. South Carolina's outbreak from October 2025 to April 2026 totaled 997 cases. The Centers for Disease Control (CDC) said 3,887 total measles cases have been reported in the U.S. this year as of Oct. 1. The agency also noted that there have been 42 new outbreaks reported this year. Never miss a story — sign up for PEOPLE's free daily newsletter to stay up-to-date on the best of what PEOPLE has to offer, from celebrity news to compelling human interest stories. CDC guidance calls for two doses of the MMR vaccine during childhood to be vaccinated against measles. The first is recommended at 12 to 15 months, followed by another dose between the ages of 4 to 6. The agency said the two doses are 97% effective against measles. After Pennsylvania announced its fifth death due to measles, the CDC told ABC News in a statement that it "has mounted an aggressive response to measles outbreaks across the country." "Texas, South Carolina, Virginia, Kansas, New Mexico, and Wisconsin requested CDC Epi-Aids and worked alongside federal teams to help bring their outbreaks rapidly and successfully under control," the agency said, adding, that the "CDC has not received a formal Epi-Aid request from Pennsylvania" and "if requested, CDC stands ready to deploy epidemiologists to support the state's identified needs." Time
The Pennsylvania Department of Health reported the measles outbreak update on Monday, Oct. The outbreak began in April 2026, and as of Oct. 5, 2026, 1,004 cases were recorded. Last week alone, 106 cases were reported.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in Pennsylvania, USA. The affected population includes individuals of all ages, with 32% of diagnosed cases being under 18 years old. The health department noted that fewer than 1% of those diagnosed were fully vaccinated.
Event
A measles outbreak in Pennsylvania has reached 1,004 total cases, making it the largest in the U.S. The outbreak has resulted in 198 hospitalizations and five deaths. The Pennsylvania Department of Health reported these figures, and infectious disease experts have warned that the outbreak may continue for several more months.
Scale, impact and risk
5, 2026, 1,004 measles cases have been recorded in Pennsylvania, surpassing South Carolina's outbreak. Health experts have stated that low vaccination rates are contributing to the spread of the virus, and the CDC has noted that 3,887 measles cases have been reported in the U.S. this year, with 42 new outbreaks.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC has stated that it has mounted an aggressive response to measles outbreaks across the country and has not received a formal Epi-Aid request from Pennsylvania. However, the CDC has indicated it is ready to deploy epidemiologists if needed. The Pennsylvania Department of Health is actively monitoring the outbreak and has shared updates with the media.

官员称阿勒格尼县的 2 起 麻疹 病例相互关联

Officials say 2 Allegheny County measles cases are related
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州阿勒格尼县的官员证实,两起 麻疹 病例相关,第二起病例可能与学校暴露有关。阿勒格尼县卫生部门表示,该感染与另一例确诊病例有关,尽管此前声称没有与学校相关的接触。受影响的人是未接种疫苗的未成年人,而学校 Eden Christian Academy 的 疫苗接种 率较低,有超过 550 名学生未接种疫苗。卫生部门正在努力确定其他暴露情况,并正在联系那些可能受到影响的人。学校正在配合调查,但尚未透露具体暴露地点。官员们强调了确定个人如何被感染以防止进一步传播的重要性。暴发 强调了由于 疫苗接种 比率下降而导致的 麻疹 风险,该比率低于群体免疫所需的阈值。卫生部门正在向没有健康保险的人免费提供 麻疹、腮腺炎和风疹 (MMR) 疫苗,以解决这一问题。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 10 月 9 日星期五(发布日期);证据中没有明确说明事件日期。
地点与对象
宾夕法尼亚州阿勒格尼县,特别是北山的伊甸基督教学院,未接种疫苗的未成年人是受影响人群。
事件
官员们证实阿勒格尼县的两起 麻疹 病例相关,其中第二起病例可能与学校接触有关,尽管此前声称没有与学校相关的接触。阿勒格尼县卫生部门报告了这一事件。
规模、影响与风险
两例确诊的 麻疹 病例,第二例与学校接触有关。该学校的 疫苗接种 率较低,有超过 550 名学生未接种疫苗。卫生部门警告称存在进一步传播的风险,并强调需要确定其他暴露情况。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生部门正在对可能接触者进行接触者追踪和外展活动。学校正在与卫生部门合作。暴露的具体地点尚未透露,调查正在进行中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Officials say 2 Allegheny County measles cases are related

News Brief
Officials in Allegheny County, Pennsylvania, confirmed that two measles cases are related, with the second case likely linked to a school exposure. The Allegheny County Health Department stated that the infection is connected to a separate confirmed case, despite earlier claims of no school-related exposures. The affected individuals are unvaccinated minors, and the school, Eden Christian Academy, has low vaccination rates, with over 550 students unvaccinated. The health department is working to identify additional exposures and is contacting those potentially affected. The school is cooperating with the investigation, but it has not disclosed the specific location of the exposure. Officials emphasized the importance of determining how the individuals were infected to prevent further spread. The outbreak highlights the risk of measles due to declining vaccination rates, which are below the threshold needed for herd immunity. The health department is offering free measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccines to those without health insurance to address the issue.
Original source text (English)

Newsroom Technical Support Health Now Officials say 2 Allegheny County measles cases are related Eden Christian Academy in the North Hills says it is affected By James Engel 3 Min Read • Oct. 9, 2026 | 47 seconds ago Go Ad-Free today A lab test for measles comes back positive. (Adobe Stock) The Allegheny County Health Department said Friday a second county resident who has tested positive for measles was likely exposed while at school. The department said the infection is related to a separate confirmed case announced Monday — the first involving a county resident — despite the fact that officials previously said there were “no school-related exposures.” “They do not live in the same household, which suggests there was likely a previously unknown exposure at the school,” the department said in a statement. Ronnie Das, a spokesman for the department, declined to say at which school the exposure took place. But Eden Christian Academy, which operates three campuses in the North Hills — elementary schools in Ross and Pine and an “upper school” in Ohio Township — said in a statement it was “recently notified by local public health authorities of measles within our school community.” The school said its policy and privacy regulations barred it from disclosing information about the person who was infected. But, the statement said, “We are working directly and in full cooperation with the Allegheny County Health Department and implementing their protocol to mitigate further spread.” The academy enrolls more than 800 students, according to its website. Measles vaccination rates among Eden Christian Academy’s student body are far below the roughly 95% required for herd immunity against the disease. At Eden’s elementary schools, only about 83% of students were immunized, according to county data from last school year. That figure was closer to 90% at its upper school, though only 80% of students there had all required vaccinations. Between all its campuses, more than 550 students were not vaccinated against measles last year. Only one student, however, filed for a medical exemption to vaccination; the rest were religious or moral objectors. Officials described both infected individuals in the county as unvaccinated minors. The county previously said that anyone who visited the Target at Pine Creek Shopping Center in McCandless (9805 McKnight Road) between 3:45 and 5:45 p.m. Thursday, Oct. 1, may have been exposed. “This connection significantly expands our investigation,” Dr. Jillian Irwin, the county health department medical director, said in a statement. “Determining how these individuals were infected and the details of all additional exposures is key to successfully stopping further spread.” The department said it will call or text those who may have been exposed. Confirmed cases of measles — 1,136 across the state as of Friday, according to Pennsylvania Health Department data — had already been recorded in neighboring Westmoreland and Armstrong counties, although before this week, no infections were noted in Pennsylvania’s second largest population center. The outbreak has yet to result in any confirmed cases in Philadelphia. The state health department has so far reported five measles-related deaths since late August. Health experts previously told TribLive, however, they expect the number of confirmed cases — which most already consider a vast undercount — to continue to rise as respiratory virus season begins. The cases have been driven by a drop in vaccination rates, which now are below the numbers required for herd immunity. A highly contagious virus, measles spreads “when an infected person breaths, coughs or sneezes. The virus remains in the air and on surfaces for up to two hours, even after the infected person leaves the area.” The county health department provides free measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccines to people without health insurance. Its immunization clinic is at 425 First Ave. in Downtown Pittsburgh. Call 412-578-8062 to make an appointment. Allegheny County, Pennsylvania, specifically Eden Christian Academy in the North Hills, with unvaccinated minors as the affected population.
Event
Officials confirmed two measles cases in Allegheny County are related, with the second case likely linked to a school exposure despite previous claims of no school-related exposures. The Allegheny County Health Department reported this event.
Scale, impact and risk
Two confirmed measles cases, with the second case linked to a school exposure. The school has low vaccination rates, with over 550 students unvaccinated. The health department warned of the risk of further spread and emphasized the need to identify additional exposures.
Response, status and uncertainty
The health department is conducting contact tracing and outreach to those potentially exposed. The school is cooperating with the health department. The exact location of the exposure is not disclosed, and the investigation is ongoing.

在受 麻疹 影响的威斯康星州社区中,对 疫苗 的看法仍然褒贬不一

In Wisconsin communities affected by measles, opinions on vaccines remain mixed
新闻简报In Wisconsin, a measles outbreak has been ongoing since early August 2026, with 240 cases reported across Grant, Iowa, Lafayette, and Vernon counties. Vernon County has the lowest measles vaccination rate in the state, with only 42 percent of 2-year-olds vaccinated last year, far below the 95 percent needed for herd immunity. The outbreak has sparked mixed opinions on vaccines among residents, with some questioning the necessity of childhood immunizations. Health officials are urging vaccination, particularly the MMR vaccine, to curb the spread of the virus. The CDC highlights that the MMR vaccine is very safe, with serious reactions being extremely rare, and that contracting measles poses significant risks, including pneumonia, brain swelling, and death. Despite these assurances, skepticism persists, and the situation remains under investigation. The outbreak has not significantly disrupted daily life, but concerns about long-term vaccine impacts and community health continue to grow.
查看新闻五要素
时间
其他人质疑他们所看到的一长串童年照片。
地点与对象
Vernon County, Wisconsin, with 240 cases reported in Grant, Iowa, Lafayette, and Vernon counties. The vaccination rate for 2-year-olds in Vernon County was 42 percent last year, compared to 80 percent statewide and the required 95 percent for herd immunity.
事件
自 2026 年 8 月初以来,麻疹 的 麻疹 一直在威斯康星州西南部持续进行,四个县报告了 240 例病例。暴发 导致居民对 疫苗 的看法不一,其中一些人质疑童年 疫苗接种 的必要性。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致 240 例确诊病例,卫生官员对病毒传播表示担忧。CDC强调,麻疹 疫苗比感染这种疾病更安全,这种疾病可能导致肺炎、脑肿胀和死亡等严重并发症。暴发 正在进行中,如果 疫苗接种 利率不增加,则存在进一步蔓延的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员鼓励 疫苗接种,特别是 MMR 疫苗,帮助结束 暴发。不过,一些居民仍持怀疑态度,情况仍在调查中。CDC 表示 MMR 疫苗 非常安全,但公共卫生措施的有效性仍在评估中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

In Wisconsin communities affected by measles, opinions on vaccines remain mixed

News Brief
In Wisconsin, a measles outbreak has been ongoing since early August 2026, with 240 cases reported across Grant, Iowa, Lafayette, and Vernon counties. Vernon County has the lowest measles vaccination rate in the state, with only 42 percent of 2-year-olds vaccinated last year, far below the 95 percent needed for herd immunity. The outbreak has sparked mixed opinions on vaccines among residents, with some questioning the necessity of childhood immunizations. Health officials are urging vaccination, particularly the MMR vaccine, to curb the spread of the virus. The CDC highlights that the MMR vaccine is very safe, with serious reactions being extremely rare, and that contracting measles poses significant risks, including pneumonia, brain swelling, and death. Despite these assurances, skepticism persists, and the situation remains under investigation. The outbreak has not significantly disrupted daily life, but concerns about long-term vaccine impacts and community health continue to grow.

Others question what they see as a lengthy list of childhood shots. By Hope Kirwan October 9, 2026 Updated October 9, 2026 at 1:13 pm Download Read Transcript Pedestrians walk through downtown Viroqua, Wis., on Tuesday, Oct. 6, 2026. Angela Major/WPR Tattoo Zuzu sits on Viroqua’s busy Main Street, with an eclectic mix of art and thrifted treasures filling the front of the shop. The name is in honor of owner Audra Grosz’s 5-year-old daughter, a nickname Grosz’s mother loved from “It’s a Wonderful Life.” Her daughter was a big part of why Grosz and her husband moved from Madison to Viroqua four and a half years ago. She said they were looking for the slower pace of life that comes with living in a city of 4,500 people, and she now appreciates how her neighbors help each other and support local businesses. Understanding Wisconsin, Together. WPR’s “Wisconsin Today” newsletter keeps you connected to the state you love without feeling overwhelmed. No paywall. No agenda. No corporate filter. X/Twitter This field is for validation purposes and should be left unchanged. Email The tight-knit community is also located in one of the southwestern Wisconsin counties experiencing an outbreak of measles since early August. There have been 240 cases in the outbreak so far across Grant, Iowa, Lafayette and Vernon counties. Wisconsin now has the fourth-highest number of cases of the highly contagious and potentially deadly disease in the country, according to the latest data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Vernon County, where Viroqua is located, has by far the lowest rate of vaccinations against measles in the state. Only 42 percent of 2-year-olds had the shot last year, according to data from the Wisconsin Department of Health Services. By comparison, just under 80 percent of all Wisconsin 2-year-olds were vaccinated last year. But that’s still well below the 95 percent that health experts say is needed to keep the virus from spreading. Audra Grosz, owner of Tattoo Zuzu in Viroqua, said she’s unsurprised Vernon County has the lowest measles vaccination rate in the state, given the diverse views on vaccines among her neighbors Hope Kirwan/WPR Grosz said the city and surrounding county is home to an eclectic mix of people — many of whom harbor skepticism about childhood vaccinations, though for different reasons. “It’s kind of a mix of Amish, Libertarian, secular communities, MAHA,” she said, referencing the “Make America Healthy Again” slogan used by federal Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. “Everyone’s here. A bunch of hippies and a bunch of farmers.” Residents of the measles-affected counties told WPR that life has largely carried on like normal in recent weeks, and that people aren’t likely to talk about the outbreak outside of their own like-minded circle. But views on vaccines, even among insular groups like the Amish, are far from black and white. Health officials say there’s new interest in getting the measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine, which they hope can help end the outbreak before it leads to a patient’s death. A pedestrian walks through downtown Viroqua, Wis., on Tuesday, Oct. 6, 2026. Angela Major/WPR Vaccine choice, but at a cost The decision to get her daughter vaccinated was a “no-brainer” for Grosz and her husband, she said. The state’s vaccination requirements were also a big reason they chose to enroll their daughter in the public school system instead of the local private school. “In the wake of COVID, there needs to be some sort of ‘keep everyone safe’ policy,” she said. But an increasing number of families are opting out of Wisconsin’s student vaccination using waivers for religious beliefs and personal convictions. State data shows 6.5 percent of students across Wisconsin had a personal conviction waiver for one or more vaccines last school year, a record high that follows a steady increase for several decades. Just over 1 percent of students had a waiver for religious beliefs, a category that has seen a much slower increase in the past five years. Viroqua resident Lori Dehlin said she remembers signing the vaccination waivers for her five daughters, all of whom are now in their 20s. Dehlin said she had all of her children get the standard childhood shots, including MMR, though not on the state-required timeline. But she did not feel comfortable with newer shots like the one for the human papillomavirus, or HPV, which came out in 2006. Lori Dehlin from Viroqua said she thinks it’s a good thing that parents feel they have a choice in which vaccines their child gets. Hope Kirwan/WPR As new vaccines have become available in recent years, Dehlin said she often questions how much she needs another shot and whether it’s been around long enough to know long-term health impacts. “Maybe that is why parents are questioning things more, because they’re throwing so much at you,” she said. Dehlin thinks parents should be able to make their own choices about which vaccinations their children should get. But she also acknowledged people’s choices have contributed to the resurgence of diseases like measles, which was declared eliminated in the U.S. in 2000. If the highly contagious virus continues to spread, Dehlin said she hopes parents will differentiate MMR from newer shots, especially given its more than 50 years of use. “It’s gone through a whole generation, practically, so to me, it’s safe,” Dehlin said. “I’ve made it to being a grandmother, and I’m still OK from getting the measles vaccine.” A nurse, prepares a combination measles, mumps and rubella vaccine for a patient at a pediatrics clinic in Easley, S.C., March 17, 2026. AP Photo/Mary Conlon, File The combination measles, mumps and rubella vaccine is “very safe” according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Serious reactions to the shot are extremely rare, and the CDC emphasizes on its website that getting the vaccine is much safer than contracting measles, especially for children under age 5. Serious complications from measles can include pneumonia, swelling of the brain and even death. Despite these assurances, skepticism about the vaccine persists. Charles Angell, another Viroqua resident, said he knows parents in the community who have chosen not to have their children vaccinated against measles. Angell said the MMR vaccine and other shots like polio have years of evidence and studies to back them up. But he thinks the total number of vaccines now recommended for kids is “pretty excessive.” “Do we really understand what we’re doing to our bodies over the course of the long-term by exposing us to those kind of external influences?” Angell questioned, adding that he’s not convinced by the safety assurances made by federal agencies and national medical organizations. Viroqua resident Charles Angell said he worries about the long-term impacts of adopting a variety of vaccines as part of routine health care. Hope Kirwan/WPR Outbreak inspires some to get the shot The recent outbreak may be changing some residents’ minds about vaccination. Wisconsin Immunization Program Manager Stephanie Schauer told reporters during a recent press conference that local health departments in the measles-affected counties have seen new interest in the MMR vaccine from people who had previously skipped the shot. Sitting in a waiting room at Upland Hills Hospital in Dodgeville, Dr. Sarah Fox said she has also seen more parents who have previously been hesitant about vaccinations getting the shot for their children. Dodgeville is the county seat of Iowa County, where 74 cases in the outbreak have been confirmed. For some patients, Fox thinks measles hasn’t felt like an immediate risk, even as outbreaks have popped up in other states. She said other parents have pushed back on the MMR shot because of misinformation. Dr. Sarah Fox, family medicine provider for Upland Hills Health, said the measles outbreak has prompted patients who have previously been hesitant to get the shot. Hope Kirwan/WPR “There was some false information that was out about it for many years,” said Fox, who is a family medicine provider. “I’m really not hearing that at this point. I think that the vast majority of my patients are getting vaccinated against MMR, and a lot of parents are actually looking forward to the day when their child is old enough to get that vaccine.” This false information about the MMR vaccine has not only been spread online but also by the HHS secretary himself. Before his 2024 presidential campaign, Kennedy spent years as the leader of an anti-vaccine group that made false claims and promoted skepticism about vaccines’ safety. In a podcast interview during that campaign, he said there is “no vaccine that is ... safe and effective,” something he later falsely denied saying. After becoming part of the Trump administration, in media interviews during the 2025 measles outbreak in Texas, he falsely claimed the MMR vaccine contains “a lot of aborted fetus debris and DNA particles.” Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., speaks at the Republican convention, Sept. 10, 2026, in Dallas. AP Photo/Alex Brandon, File This claim likely comes from the fact that the rubella portion of the vaccine is developed by growing the virus in fetal cells that originated from two elective abortions in the early 1960s. The virus is then purified to ensure no DNA or cellular debris remain before being manufactured into a vaccine. Questions surround Plain communities When a handful of cases was first found in Iowa and Lafayette counties in August, Fox said she knew measles would “spread like wildfire.” She immediately started making plans about how her primary care clinic would communicate with the local health department and field an influx of patient calls. The measles virus is highly contagious, and 9 out of 10 unprotected people who are exposed will become infected, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. In addition to infants who are too young to be vaccinated, Fox said she was most concerned about community groups in the area that have little to no vaccination coverage. Due to patient privacy, public health officials have not shared who in the affected counties have been hit by measles. But some health providers and experts have speculated that at least some of the cases are in people who belong to local Amish and Mennonite communities. The Christian groups, which are collectively known as Plain communities, are known for their traditional dress, use of horse and buggy and limited use of technology. An estimated 3,000 Amish people live across Grant, Iowa and Lafayette counties. Mark Louden, interpreter and cultural liaison for many of the state’s Plain communities, said many have family ties to Amish communities in Pennsylvania’s Lancaster County, where a significant measles outbreak has led to 155 hospitalizations and four deaths. Louden said health care choices are left up to individuals and families in the Plain communities. But in general, community members are less likely to use preventive health care and tend to be selective about which vaccines they choose to receive. People in Plain communities are also hesitant to speak with the media due to their beliefs about modesty and humility. WPR stopped at an Amish greenhouse in Lafayette County with an invitation to speak about the ongoing measles outbreak. The person working declined an interview but said her family “had the shots” and hasn’t been affected by the virus. Mineral Point resident Matt Renwick said the outbreak has inspired him to talk to his teenage kids about the safety and importance of vaccines. Hope Kirwan/WPR ‘Every parent I’ve met wants to do the right thing’ Mineral point resident Matt Renwick said he’s been digging through a large box of old photos and school report cards saved by his parents in search of the paper booklet containing his childhood vaccination records. “What’s hard is that I moved around a lot when I was a kid, so I don’t know,” Renwick said. “I’m still looking, but if I’m a close contact because I work in some schools, and if I can’t find documentation, I’d have to get my two measles shots again. That was not on my bingo card for this past year, but, you know, here we are.” Renwick is a former elementary school principal who works as an educational consultant. He led his school through the COVID-19 pandemic, testing kids when the school nurse was overwhelmed and fielding vaccine questions after the shots became available. So he feels for the local school leaders who are having to navigate the current outbreak. “This doesn’t seem as big, because just a lot of people had the vaccines already,” he said. “But it just takes away the focus of the schools. (It forces schools) to have to manage one more thing that really isn’t the school’s responsibility but gets thrust upon the schools.” When he was a principal, Renwick said he tried to always be respectful when having conversations with parents about vaccines, even if their concerns were based on misinformation. But personally, he is frustrated with federal leaders who have sowed doubt about the safety of vaccines and the ability to trust institutions. “Every parent I’ve met wants to do the right thing by their child,” he said. “When you have an information ecosystem that is more fractured than it used to be, and you’re not really sure who to or what to believe, that can be very difficult.” Because his two teenage kids are vaccinated, Renwick said his family has largely carried on with their normal lives. But he said the outbreak has prompted him to try to talk to his kids about measles and vaccines, hoping to counteract any misinformation they may come across on social media. Renwick admitted that he has been hesitant to draw attention to his views. But he hoped that voicing his trust in the safety of the MMR vaccine could help others in his small community to do the same. “I feel like this is something I’m doing that’s responsible,” he said. “Just taking action and making my voice heard. It’s not just my kids or my wife, I want everyone here to be safe and healthy. And if you get a few negative comments toward you, I think it’s worth it.” Transcript Expand Collapse Transcript Terms of Use Transcripts are provided for accessibility and convenience and may be created using a mix of automated tools and human review. Over the last two months, measles has spread to more than 200 people across rural southwestern Wisconsin. Public health officials have been rushing to get ahead of the highly contagious virus, but with low rates of vaccination in the region, especially in concentrated communities, it’s not clear when the outbreak will end. Hope Kerwin recently hit the road to talk with residents in the measles-affected counties. They shared how life has changed and stayed the same since the virus was found in August. On Viroqua’s busy Main Street, Audra Gross welcomes me into her tattoo shop. She and her husband moved from the crowds of Madison to the small town in rural Vernon County more than four years ago. We had a one-and-a-half-year-old, so we wanted life to slow down. Gross loves her tight-knit community, but it’s also home to an eclectic mix of people, many of whom are skeptical about childhood vaccinations for various reasons. It’s kind of a mix of Amish, libertarian, secular communities, Maha. Everyone’s here. Vernon County is the latest to be hit by the state’s measles outbreak this fall, which started in Iowa, Lafayette, and Grant counties. With more than 200 cases, Wisconsin has the fourth highest number of measles infections in the U.S. this year. But residents in the affected counties say the outbreak hasn’t changed daily life, or the fact that most people aren’t interested in talking about measles outside of their like-minded circles. For Gross and her husband, it was an easy decision to get their daughter vaccinated against measles mumps and rubella, or MMR. 宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 暴发 对全美怀孕护理意味着什么 - 19 新闻

What Pennsylvania’s measles outbreak could mean for pregnancy care across America - The 19th News
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 暴发 已感染超过 975 人和至少 50 名孕妇,其中 5 人死亡,其中包括两名婴儿。以兰卡斯特县为中心的 暴发 引起了人们对先天性 麻疹 风险的担忧,以及随着 疫苗接种 发病率下降对美国妊娠护理的影响。医生强调孕前疫苗接种对于保护母亲和婴儿的重要性。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部正在追踪病例,但由于隐私法的原因,无法确认先天性 麻疹 病例的数量。他们正在与疾病预防控制中心合作开发跟踪系统。暴发 强调了怀孕个体的脆弱性以及改进免疫测试和 疫苗接种 策略的必要性。暴发 引发了有关未接种疫苗的成年人和孕妇交叉感染的讨论,专家警告可能会出现流产、早产和低出生体重等并发症。正在优先考虑预防措施和意识,以减轻风险。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

What Pennsylvania’s measles outbreak could mean for pregnancy care across America - The 19th News

News Brief
A measles outbreak in Pennsylvania has infected over 975 people and at least 50 pregnant women, with five deaths linked to the outbreak, including two infants. The outbreak, centered in Lancaster County, has raised concerns about the risks of congenital measles and the impact on pregnancy care in the U.S. as vaccination rates decline. Doctors are emphasizing the importance of pre-pregnancy vaccination to protect both mothers and infants. The Pennsylvania Department of Health is tracking cases but cannot confirm the number of congenital measles cases due to privacy laws. They are collaborating with the CDC to develop a tracking system. The outbreak highlights the vulnerability of pregnant individuals and the need for improved immunity testing and vaccination strategies. The outbreak has prompted discussions about the intersection of unvaccinated adults and pregnant people, with experts warning of potential complications such as pregnancy loss, premature delivery, and low birth weight. Preventative measures and awareness are being prioritized to mitigate risks.

Health What Pennsylvania’s measles outbreak could mean for pregnancy care across America It's raising new questions about immunity testing before pregnancy, early vaccinations for babies and the many complications that could rise from infection. New cases of congenital measles and increasing exposure risks are putting a spotlight on the vulnerabilities of pregnancy and postpartum care in America in an era of lowering vaccination rates. (Getty Images) Barbara Rodriguez Health Reporter Published October 6, 2026, 5:00 a.m. CT 2026-10-06 05:00 5:00 October 6, 2026 am America/Chicago Republish this story Share Bluesky Facebook Email A deadly measles outbreak in Pennsylvania, which has infected more than 975 people this year and been linked to five deaths, has also infected at least 50 pregnant women in the state. Doctors in outbreak areas are making changes to how they care for pregnant patients and their babies, offering a glimpse of what may be in store across the nation as cases spread. Among the deaths were two infants in Lancaster County, an area about 60 miles west of Philadelphia and the epicenter of the Pennsylvania outbreak. A newborn had congenital measles, which was passed in utero through an unvaccinated mother who was infected. It is not clear whether the other infant, who was reportedly 6 weeks old at the time of death and had a genetic birth disorder, was exposed in utero or after birth. Some Pennsylvania-based doctors have experienced an uptick in anxious questions from patients about the implications of measles on just about all aspects of pregnancy care: What do more cases mean for a current pregnancy? Are additional precautions needed for someone planning to get pregnant soon? How concerned should parents with a newborn be about public outings? The 19th thanks our sponsors. Become one. It all depends, doctors in Pennsylvania and nationally told The 19th, on preventative care measures taken prior to pregnancy and a person’s proximity to an outbreak. What is certain is that new cases of congenital measles and increasing exposure risks are putting a spotlight on the vulnerabilities of pregnancy and postpartum care in America in an era of lowering vaccination rates. “Air doesn’t know that there’s a border between Pennsylvania and Ohio, or that there’s a border between New Jersey and New York,” said Dr. Sharee Livingston, an OBGYN in Lancaster County. “We are dealing with a respiratory illness, and even if we weren’t dealing with a respiratory illness, none of us live in a vacuum.” An evolving outbreak (Getty Images) Livingston never came across a single case of measles during her medical training in the mid-2000s, and neither did many of her peers. But in the past six months, Livingston estimates that she has treated at least a dozen patients infected with the highly contagious disease. Livingston did not share more information about the patients, including whether they were all women or if any were pregnant. The Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH) told The 19th that, as of Friday, a total of 977 measles cases had been reported in 2026, and 53 of those were among pregnant women. As of early October state health officials have reported nearly 40 percent of Pennsylvania’s cases were coming from Lancaster County. The five measles-associated deaths — the infants, a 40-year-old woman, an 18-year-old and a 20-year-old with leukemia — were the first in the state in 35 years. “Measles wasn’t a conversation we were having before, but it is now,” said Dr. Kisha Davis, president-elect of the American Academy of Family Physicians. DOH told The 19th it could not confirm any estimate of the number of congenital measles cases in the state or provide more details about the infected pregnant women, citing state privacy laws. The department said there is no nationally standardized definition for congenital measles and that it is working with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention to develop a system for tracking and reporting cases. They have been tracking cases during pregnancy and in “very young children” and said that since Oct. 2, more than 100 measles cases in the state have been in children under the age of 4. NBC News reported that at least six newborns in the state have been infected with measles during the outbreak, citing a Pennsylvania doctor. There have been more than 3,800 confirmed measles cases in the United States this year, the highest number in decades. Roughly 95 percent of the infections are among unvaccinated people or individuals whose vaccination status is unknown. It is unclear how many of those people were pregnant or postpartum at the time of infection. But Dr. Kevin Ault, an OBGYN who recently started working at Penn State Health in Hershey — about 30 miles northwest of Lancaster County — noted that more than a quarter of cases were in adults of childbearing age. “At any given time, about 1 percent of Americans are pregnant. So there’s an intersection there of unvaccinated adults and pregnant people that are going to be in this scenario,” said Ault, who is a fellow with the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) and serves in the organization’s expert group on infectious diseases and immunization. Doctors do not recommend that pregnant patients get the measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) vaccine because it carries a live virus, making immunization prior to pregnancy critical. Vaccinated pregnant people pass antibodies to their infants and have an immune system that is better positioned to keep a future fetus and baby safe from measles or to reduce complications in the rare event of a breakthrough infection. Livingston has been trying to reassure vaccinated patients who may still worry about infections, reminding them of these robust protections. But, she noted, “if the pregnant person believes that they’ve had an exposure and they’re unvaccinated, that’s where it becomes a problem. Because then there’s a risk to both the fetus and the mother.” A ‘disconcerting’ reality (Getty Images) An infection in an unvaccinated person during pregnancy has a host of complications including pregnancy loss, premature delivery and low birth weight. New guidelines on congenital measles released last month by the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) warn of a 25 to 30 percent rate of infection if measles is passed through the birth parent within days of a birth. The guidelines had been in the works for some time but carried more urgency after the infant fatalities in Pennsylvania, said Dr. Pia Pannaraj, a member of the AAP’s Committee on Infectious Diseases and a professor of pediatrics at the University of California, San Diego. “Many doctors that are practicing right now might never have seen measles in their lifetime, so they know it from the textbooks, but that’s not the same as having a patient,” she said. “So we’re having to educate providers: ‘How do you recognize measles? This is what the rash looks like.’” The AAP guidelines break down different scenarios for monitoring congenital and newborn measles infections. In some cases, babies without overt symptoms of measles birthed by an infected parent can be given a post-exposure antibody known as immune globulin — but it may not help if infection has already occurred. Treatment in general for infected babies is limited and includes monitoring possible complications, like a fever or pneumonia. “There’s only basically what we call supportive care,” Pannaraj said. “So we treat the symptoms as they arrive as best we can, but there’s no way to basically kill the measles virus, and there’s no way to prevent the complications that will occur.” Infected babies can be at higher risk for encephalitis, a type of brain swelling, as well as a rare but fatal brain inflammation that can appear later in life. “Mom takes the hit of getting the disease, but the infants are at the highest risk for measles complications of anybody that could get measles,” Pannaraj added, noting that babies’ immune systems are incredibly underdeveloped. Dr. Holly Cummings, an OBGYN in Philadelphia and the chair of Pennsylvania’s section of ACOG, said she’s increasingly ordering bloodwork to check the measles immunity of patients who are thinking about getting pregnant. This hasn’t always been Cummings’ approach. She already assesses her patients’ immunity for other viruses but relied on the general understanding that most people have received the MMR series, which is 97 percent effective at preventing measles. Several doctors told The 19th that they’ve worked under an assumption that children growing up in the 1990s and onward went through public schools that mandated the MMR vaccine. Those mandates are still in place, but exemption laws have been expanded since then. ACOG, which has guidelines on measles that it developed more than a decade ago, recommends that doctors assess a patient’s immunity with either proof of vaccination or through bloodwork. For patients born in the ‘90s, electronic medical records of their childhood vaccinations may also be more readily available to doctors. A measles immunity check before pregnancy may become a necessity in the future, but it will depend on circumstances like community spread and declining vaccination rates, Davis said. “The vast majority of folks are still vaccinated — the fact that we are having to have that conversation again feels a little disconcerting,” she said. These clinical considerations will carry more weight as more adults choose not to vaccinate their families, and as their children reach childbearing age. Vaccination rates for routine vaccines continue to decline among kindergartners, according to new CDC data. In Pennsylvania, more than half of counties have an MMR vaccination rate below 95 percent, the threshold for herd immunity. That includes Philadelphia County and some groups of children in Lancaster County. While some of the most impacted areas in the state are far from Philadelphia, Cummings doesn’t want to make assumptions about her patients’ vaccine status. In September, public health officials reported a measles exposure at several locations within the Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia complex, including a NICU lounge. “In the context of this current measles outbreak — and particularly for me right now given that there’s such a high number of cases in Pennsylvania — it does feel important for me to bring it up and ensure that my patients are protected,” she said. Changing vaccine landscape (Joe Raedle/Getty Images) There are implications for future care beyond the frequency of immunity testing. Doctors may reexamine how they advise parents about early MMR vaccination for their babies. Infants as young as six months old can be approved for an early MMR shot if they will be in an area with potential measles exposure. This option, Pannaraj said, used to be reserved for infants traveling internationally to high-risk exposure areas. But doctors are increasingly recommending an early shot if a child is living in or traveling domestically to an outbreak region. An early shot doesn’t negate the need for two shots later, which have been timed for maximum effectiveness. “Is there going to be a time where we need to think about earlier vaccination?” she said. “Now, I hope that’s not the case, because currently the outbreaks are still limited within the United States. We’re all working hard to make sure it stays that way and to try to get rid of the outbreak.” Emphasizing the importance of vaccination has been complicated in recent years. The politicization of pandemic-era mitigation policies gave rise to a new wave of online misinformation about the safety and efficacy of vaccines, including the MMR vaccine. Disinformation over the disputed link between the MMR vaccine and autism flourished in the late ‘90s, but vaccine disinformation has a more powerful megaphone now through the federal government. Both President Donald Trump and Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. have spread false information about routine childhood vaccines. In August, Trump claimed without evidence that the MMR shot can be “quite lethal.” And while Kennedy’s department has occasionally promoted the MMR vaccine, he falsely said last year that the shot causes annual deaths. In 2021, Kennedy gave a speech in Lancaster County where he downplayed the threat of measles. His record shapes public perception: New polling shows 40 percent of adults are unsure whether or not Kennedy recommends the MMR vaccine, and another 29 percent think he does not recommend it. Kennedy oversees the CDC, which has not recognized all five deaths reported in Pennsylvania. The department says on its measles dashboard that it is working to standardize a definition for measles deaths. “These are not new efforts. What is new today is the fact that there’s been a corporatization of the anti-vaccine movement,” Chelsea Clinton, vice chair of the Clinton Foundation and assistant professor at Columbia University’s Mailman School of Public Health, told The 19th. “There are deep profit motives frequently fueling many of the loudest voices. I think some have deeply painful personal experiences and extrapolated out of those without scientific evidence or data and yet really believe they’re trying to save other families.” Still, public health officials in Pennsylvania are seeing a response to the outbreak. In August, health providers reported administering more than 46,000 doses of the MMR vaccine — a massive jump from the roughly 25,000 doses they recorded monthly during the first half of the year. Doctors say a person who is thinking about getting pregnant should get the MMR vaccine or check their immunity through bloodwork prior to conception. Someone who is pregnant and not vaccinated should consult with health providers about any special precautions to take to avoid an infection. “We should be talking to women and to anyone whose partner may be thinking about being pregnant so that being caught up on your vaccines is just part of what someone is thinking about while also maybe trying to stop smoking, and drink more water, and eat healthier and all the other things that people often say they’re trying to optimize for their health before getting pregnant,” Clinton said. Livingston credits state and federal public health officials for being responsive to the outbreak in her county, but she sees opportunities for more equitable care that helps people walk into pregnancy the healthiest version of themselves — which in turn can help to mitigate future adverse outcomes. For now, Livingston is leaning on evidence-based best practices. She believes recent lessons from the pandemic around immunization will ultimately help her community move forward. “I don’t mean to sound really flowery or overly positive, but I am hopeful,” she said. “I believe that most people want to be healthy during their pregnancies. They care tremendously about their unborn child, and so I think when people understand that that’s our angle, it makes it a little bit easier to have the conversation and maybe adopt best practices.” Additional reporting by Isabela Espadas Barros Leal. 麻疹 病例激增后纽约州州长宣布进入紧急状态 - 卫报

New York governor declares state of emergency after spike in measles cases - The Guardian
新闻简报纽约州州长 Kathy Hochul 于 2025 年 3 月 14 日宣布进入紧急状态,以应对 麻疹 病例大幅增加,导致公共卫生危机。麻疹,特别是在纽约州的农村地区以及宾夕法尼亚州和俄亥俄州等邻近州,出现了急剧上升,其中宾夕法尼亚州经历了三十多年来最大的 麻疹 麻疹。截至 2026 年,纽约州报告了 108 例 麻疹 确诊病例,而 2023 年仅为 1 例,而宾夕法尼亚州已有 5 例死亡和 198 例住院治疗。病例激增是由于免疫不足社区(包括阿米什人)的 疫苗接种 率下降。Hochul 的紧急措施旨在扩大 疫苗 的获取和检测范围,以应对疾病的传播。美国正面临自 1991 年以来最严重的 麻疹 年,报告病例近 3,900 例。暴发 强调了 疫苗 犹豫和错误信息的影响,促使采取紧急公共卫生应对措施。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

New York governor declares state of emergency after spike in measles cases - The Guardian

News Brief
New York governor Kathy Hochul declared a state of emergency on 14 March 2025 in response to a significant increase in measles cases, which has led to a public health crisis. The outbreak, particularly in rural areas of New York and neighboring states like Pennsylvania and Ohio, has seen a dramatic rise, with Pennsylvania experiencing its largest measles outbreak in over three decades. As of 2026, New York has reported 108 confirmed measles cases, up from just one in 2023, while Pennsylvania has seen five deaths and 198 hospitalizations. The surge in cases is attributed to decreased vaccination rates in underimmunized communities, including the Amish population. Hochul’s emergency measures aim to expand vaccine access and testing, addressing the spread of the disease. The US is facing its worst measles year since 1991, with nearly 3,900 cases reported. The outbreak highlights the impact of vaccine hesitancy and misinformation, prompting urgent public health responses.

Photograph: Shannon Stapleton/Reuters View image in fullscreen A measles alert sign hangs outside a children’s hospital in New Hyde Park, New York, on 14 March 2025. Photograph: Shannon Stapleton/Reuters New York governor declares state of emergency after spike in measles cases The deadly outbreak in Pennsylvania is its largest in more than three decades, and confirmed cases in New York are up The New York governor, Kathy Hochul, declared a state of emergency on Monday in response to a dramatic spike in measles cases in the state. Hochul’s administration said the emergency declaration was in response to a “growing number of measles cases across rural areas of New York and neighboring states”, including Pennsylvania, which has been grappling with a deadly outbreak that is its largest in more than three decades. The declaration expands the pool of people who can administer vaccines against measles and allows more individuals to order testing. It comes as there have been 108 confirmed cases of measles in New York so far for the year, up from just one reported case in 2023. In Pennsylvania, which neighbors New York, five people have died after contracting the disease, and there have been 198 hospitalizations. The state surpassed 1,000 measles cases on Monday while Ohio has also seen an increase. New York, Pennsylvania and Ohio have sizable Amish populations, which tend to be undervaccinated. And undervaccination has been a driving factor in measles spreading after its domestic US spread had been eliminated in 2000 by high vaccination rates. “After reviewing the latest data on measles cases in New York state, I am declaring a State Disaster Emergency to strengthen our ongoing efforts to confront this disease,” Hochul said. “No one should get seriously ill or die from a vaccine-preventable disease. That is why I have taken steps to increase the number of health professionals who can vaccinate and test. These emergency measures will protect New Yorkers and support our community partners as they work tirelessly to address the spread of this disease.” The US is experiencing its worst year for measles since 1991, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, with nearly 3,900 cases reported. Of New York’s 108 measles cases, 101 have been reported outside of New York City, the state said. Since 15 July, 92 cases have been reported in underimmunized rural communities. It is a stark increase from previous years. Beside the one reported case in 2023, there were only 15 in 2024 – and in 2025, there were a total of 48 measles cases. The Trump administration health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, has been criticized for his response to the measles outbreak. Before his appointment as health secretary, he was best known as an anti-vaccine activist. Kennedy headlined an anti-vaccine conference in Washington DC in mid-September, as measles cases surged. He told the crowd there that they had a “strong and steadfast friend at the White House”. Meanwhile, the Pennsylvania governor, Josh Shapiro, has called Kennedy “incompetent”, and the two men have clashed over how to classify measles-related deaths. “The concerning rise in measles cases is due to a decrease in childhood vaccines driven by misinformation and is causing a public health emergency,” said state senator Gustavo Rivera, who chairs the senate’s health committee. At least 95% of a community must be vaccinated against measles to prevent outbreaks. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

麻疹 全年病例数达到 4,080 例——数十年来最高

Measles cases reach 4,080 for the year — the highest in decades
新闻简报根据美国疾病控制与预防中心的数据,截至 2026 年 10 月 8 日,美国的 麻疹 暴发 已达到 35 年来的最高水平,已报告 4,080 例病例。宾夕法尼亚州是这一病例激增的中心,今年报告了 1,105 例病例,并有 5 例确诊死亡,尽管 CDC 只确认了其中两例。该州的新病例大幅增加,单周报告了 142 例,并通过推出 疫苗 临时诊所来提高免疫率来应对。南卡罗来纳州、犹他州和威斯康星州等其他州也报告了很高的病例数,每个州都有超过 200 例病例。暴发 引发了有关 疫苗 安全性的争论,卫生与公共服务部部长罗伯特·F·肯尼迪强调需要解决 疫苗 伤害问题。尽管有联邦财政支持,情况仍在调查中,美国上一次出现如此多的病例是在 1991 年。病例的增加凸显了 疫苗接种 工作的重要性以及控制 麻疹病毒 传播的挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至2026年10月8日,麻疹 暴发达到4,080例,每周增加192例。宾夕法尼亚州本周报告了 5 例新病例,使全年总数达到 1,105 例。美国上一次出现如此多的病例是在 1991 年。
地点与对象
暴发 跨越美国多个州,其中宾夕法尼亚州是主要热点地区。其他出现重大病例的州包括南卡罗来纳州、犹他州和威斯康星州。今年,除五个州外,所有州都至少出现了一例 麻疹 病例,除四例外,所有州均未接种疫苗。
事件
该日历年的 麻疹 病例数十年来首次超过 4,000 例,宾夕法尼亚州有 5 例确诊死亡。截至 2026 年 10 月 8 日,CDC 报告了 4,080 例病例,宾夕法尼亚州已成为今年 暴发 的归零地。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 引起了人们对 疫苗接种 的兴趣激增,9 月份发放了超过 56,000 剂疫苗。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部门报告本周新增 142 例新病例,疾病预防控制中心仅确认该州报告的死亡病例中的两例。暴发 引起了对 疫苗 安全和公共卫生措施的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
联邦政府已向各州提供财政支持,而 CDC 则强调了 疫苗 伤害追踪的必要性。宾夕法尼亚州推出了 疫苗 临时诊所,并报告意识有所提高。目前情况仍在调查中,一些州宣布进入紧急状态,另一些州的病例数量不断上升。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases reach 4,080 for the year — the highest in decades

News Brief
The measles outbreak in the United States has reached a 35-year high, with 4,080 cases reported as of October 8, 2026, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Pennsylvania has been the epicenter of this surge, reporting 1,105 cases for the year and five confirmed deaths, though the CDC has only recognized two of these. The state has seen a significant increase in new cases, with 142 reported in a single week, and has responded by launching pop-up vaccine clinics to boost immunization rates. Other states like South Carolina, Utah, and Wisconsin also reported high numbers, with over 200 cases each. The outbreak has sparked debates about vaccine safety, with Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy emphasizing the need to address vaccine injuries. Despite federal financial support, the situation remains under investigation, and the last time the U.S. had this many cases was in 1991. The rise in cases highlights the importance of vaccination efforts and the challenges in controlling the spread of the measles virus.
Original source text (English)
A sign at a University of Utah health clinic warns visitors about the spread of measles. The state has had 535 reported cases this year, among the largest counts in the country. (Photo by McKenzie Romero/Utah News Dispatch) Measles cases for the calendar year surpassed 4,000 for the first time in decades, with as many as five deaths in Pennsylvania, as the federal government steps in with financial help for states but also fans vaccination fears and skepticism. A weekly report from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention showed 4,080 cases this year as of Oct. 8, up 192 cases in a week. Pennsylvania, which has reported five confirmed deaths from measles, alone had 142 new cases for the week — for a total of 1,105 for the year. The CDC has recognized just two of the state's reported deaths. Measles cases have reached a 35-year high Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro, a Democrat, has protested the CDC's death count; his state's five deaths would be more than the three deaths reported in 2025 for the entire nation. Other states with large numbers of cases, more than 200, did not see their case numbers change for the week: South Carolina (670 for the year), Utah (535) and Wisconsin (207). States with more cases included New York, which declared a measles emergency, with 19 new cases for a total of 103 this year; and Iowa, up 17 cases to 39. Kentucky reported separately on Friday that cases there had reached 104, up 10 from last week. Pennsylvania, which has become ground zero for this year's outbreaks, reported this week that a new awareness and pop-up vaccine clinics are leading to more measles vaccinations. All but five states have had at least one measles case this year. The last time the United States had this many cases was in 1991, reaching 9,643 cases as the nation still struggled to roll out vaccinations for the disease, amid budget cuts that hit programs in big cities. Pennsylvania's health department this week announced "a surge of interest" in vaccinations as more residents become aware of the outbreak — more than 56,000 doses were given out in September, more than twice the usual monthly volume. The state has added pop-up clinics in the most affected areas. All but four of the measles cases were among unvaccinated people. The Lewistown Sentinel reported that an 18-year-old woman there died in September from brain swelling as a result of the measles virus after two of her brothers survived the disease. While the federal government has offered states more funding to combat measles, Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy moved forward this week on plans to emphasize vaccine injury, announcing a new clinic and work to strengthen a database that tracks problems after a vaccination. "We must listen to patients and families who report vaccine injuries — not dismiss or gaslight them," Kennedy said in a statement. The measles outbreak reached 4,080 cases as of October 8, 2026, with a weekly increase of 192 cases. Pennsylvania reported five new cases for the week, bringing its total to 1,105 for the year. The last time the U.S. had this many cases was in 1991.
Location and population
The outbreak spans multiple U.S. states, with Pennsylvania being a major hotspot. Other states with significant cases include South Carolina, Utah, and Wisconsin. All but five states have had at least one measles case this year, and all but four cases were among unvaccinated individuals.
Event
Measles cases for the calendar year surpassed 4,000 for the first time in decades, with five confirmed deaths in Pennsylvania. The CDC reported 4,080 cases as of October 8, 2026, and the state of Pennsylvania has become ground zero for this year's outbreaks.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has led to a surge in vaccination interest, with over 56,000 doses given out in September. Pennsylvania's health department reported 142 new cases for the week, and the CDC has recognized only two of the state's reported deaths. The outbreak has raised concerns about vaccine safety and public health measures.
Response, status and uncertainty
The federal government has provided financial support to states, while the CDC has emphasized the need for vaccine injury tracking. Pennsylvania has launched pop-up vaccine clinics and reported increased awareness. The situation remains under investigation, with some states declaring emergencies and others experiencing rising case numbers.

本周阿勒格尼县学校出现第二例 麻疹 确诊病例

Allegheny County school site of second confirmed measles case this week
新闻简报在宾夕法尼亚州阿勒格尼县,一周内报告了两例 麻疹 确诊病例,两人就读于同一所私立基督教学校。卫生部门将一例病例与学校联系起来,另一例病例与罗斯镇一家塔吉特商店的潜在接触史有关。官员们正在调查 暴发,并建议接触者在前往设施之前联系医疗保健提供者。截至 2026 年 10 月 9 日,宾夕法尼亚州已确诊 1,078 例 麻疹 病例,到 2026 年将有 5 例死亡,207 例住院治疗。超过 99% 的病例涉及未接种疫苗的个体,这强调了未完全免疫的人面临的风险。卫生部门正在与学校合作控制疫情传播,并将每天更新病例数。由于隐私问题,有关第二个病例的详细信息(包括年龄和 疫苗接种 状态)仍未公开。
