B routine priorityPublic-health news

Allegheny County school site of second confirmed measles case this week

News Brief
In Allegheny County, Pennsylvania, two confirmed measles cases were reported within a week, with both individuals attending the same private Christian school. The health department linked one case to the school and another to potential exposure at a Target store in Ross Township. Officials are investigating the outbreak and advising exposed individuals to contact healthcare providers before visiting facilities. As of October 9, 2026, Pennsylvania has 1,078 confirmed measles cases, with five deaths and 207 hospitalizations in 2026. Over 99% of cases involved unvaccinated individuals, emphasizing the risk for those not fully immunized. The health department is working with the school to manage the spread and will update the case count daily. Details about the second case, including age and vaccination status, remain undisclosed due to privacy concerns.
Original source text (English)
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials Blueprint WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services PITTSBURGH Measles Add Topic Allegheny County school site of second confirmed measles case this week Finch Walker USA TODAY NETWORK Updated Oct. 9, 2026, 10:31 a.m. ET Hear this story Two residents in Allegheny County, Pennsylvania, have tested positive for measles. One case is linked to a private Christian school, while another involved potential exposure at a Target in Ross Township. Health officials advise those who may have been exposed to contact their healthcare provider before visiting a facility. This article was updated to include information from Allegheny County Health Department. In less than a week, two Allegheny County residents have tested positive for measles. On Oct. 5, the county health department said an unvaccinated minor had been sickened by the virus causing an outbreak that's left five people dead in Pennsylvania. Then, on Oct. 8, the agency confirmed that a second person had contracted the virus. Both attend the same school, so it's likely a previous exposure went undetected at the school, the health department said. "This connection significantly expands our investigation," said Dr. Jillian Irwin, Allegheny County Health Department's medical director. "Determining how these individuals were infected and the details of all additional exposures is key to successfully stopping further spread." Here's what to know. Who has measles in Allegheny County? A second Allegheny County resident tested positive for measles, the county health department said on Oct. 8. The individual is not in the same family as the first measles case, but both people attend the same school, which means there was likely a previously unknown exposure at the school, the department said in an Oct. 9 release. The health department did not provide any information about the second individual's age, vaccination status or what specific school they attend, though Pittsburgh's Public Source reported that the individual was a child at a private Christian school in the North Hills. Earlier in the week, an unvaccinated minor tested positive for measles, according to an Oct. 5 release from Allegheny County Health Department. They did not provide any additional information about the individual, citing privacy concerns. The number of measles cases in Allegheny County will be updated daily as needed online. Was I exposed to measles in Allegheny County? A spokesperson for Allegheny County Health Department told Pittsburgh's Public Source that the department was working with school officials at Eden Christian Academy to contain the spread, with spokesperson Ronnie Das saying the health department has "been providing specific guidance to the school nurse and administration of an impacted school on how to organize lists of immune staff and students and ensuring school staff understands who needs to quarantine and for how long." The department will call or text people who were exposed to the two measles cases while the two children were contagious, according to the Oct. 9 release. Eden Christian Academy has elementary school campuses in Ross and Pine, as well as a school for older children in Ohio Township. A spokesperson for the school didn't say which campus was impacted. While there were no school-related exposures involving the first measles case, other locals could have come into contact with the virus, according to the health department. Shoppers at the Ross Township Target, located at 9805 McKnight Road, may have been exposed to measles on Thursday, Oct. 1 between 3:45 p.m. and 5:45 p.m. What should I do if I was exposed to measles? If you believe you may have been exposed to measles, you should talk to your healthcare provider about what to do, the county health department said. Do not go to a healthcare facility without calling ahead. Those who are unvaccinated are most at risk of catching measles. Anyone who is partially vaccinated, meaning they received one dose of the MMR vaccine rather than the recommended two, may still be susceptible to the disease. Two doses of the vaccine are about 97% effective in preventing infection, according to the Centers for Disease Control. Allegheny County is recommending that healthcare providers administer the MMR vaccine on an accelerated schedule to children ages six months and older due to the outbreak. If you were vaccinated in Pennsylvania, you can check your vaccination status on the state department of health's website. If you have not been vaccinated, you should schedule your shots with your healthcare provider or the immunization clinic in Downton Pittsburgh, the county health department said. If you're uncertain if you've been vaccinated, there's no risk in getting an additional dose of the MMR vaccine. What are the symptoms of measles? How does measles spread? Measles spreads when a person infected with the virus breathes, coughs or sneezes. It spreads through the air and on surfaces, and it can remain in the air and on surfaces for up to two hours after the person has left an area. The illness starts with fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes, and is then followed by a rash that begins on the head or face and spreads downward. Symptoms can show up seven to 21 days after exposure, and a person is contagious four days before and four days after the rash begins. How many measles cases are there in Pennsylvania? As of Oct. 9 morning, there were 1,078 confirmed cases of measles throughout Pennsylvania, thought Pennsylvania Department of Health was expected to update that number later in the afternoon. Across the state, there had been five measles-associated deaths, and 207 people had been hospitalized with measles in 2026. More than 99% of all cases occurred in people who were not fully vaccinated. Finch Walker is the Pittsburgh Connect Reporter for the USA TODAY Network. Contact Walker at FWalker@usatodayco.com. Pennsylvania health officials brace for expected spread of measles outbreak - The Guardian
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州报告了 麻疹 暴发,特别影响兰开斯特县的阿米什社区,该县的 疫苗接种 比率历来较低。截至上周,该州已记录 39 个县的 943 例确诊病例和 5 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。卫生官员正在为这种疾病的传播做好准备,并指出它可能会蔓延到非阿米什社区。努力包括追踪接触者、建立 疫苗接种 中心以及解决 疫苗 错误信息。然而,许多县缺乏地方卫生部门,给应对工作带来了挑战。切斯特县有 83 例病例,正在为兰开斯特的病例涌入做准备,而蒙哥马利县的医疗主任警告说,宾夕法尼亚州人口第三多的县最终将出现病例。官员们强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,特别是对于保险不足和未参保居民而言,并正在与社区组织合作,确保获得 疫苗。尽管做出了这些努力,但由于该州基础设施有限以及人们对 疫苗 的怀疑不断增长,麻疹 的传播仍然令人担忧。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania health officials brace for expected spread of measles outbreak - The Guardian

News Brief
A measles outbreak has been reported in Pennsylvania, particularly affecting the Amish community in Lancaster County, which has historically low vaccination rates. As of last week, the state has recorded 943 confirmed cases and five measles-associated deaths across 39 counties. Health officials are bracing for the spread of the disease, noting that it may reach non-Amish communities. Efforts include contact tracing, establishing vaccination centers, and addressing vaccine misinformation. However, the lack of local health departments in many counties poses challenges to the response. Chester County, with 83 cases, is preparing for an influx from Lancaster, while Montgomery County's medical director warns that the third most populous county in Pennsylvania will eventually see cases. Officials emphasize the importance of vaccination, especially for underinsured and uninsured residents, and are working with community organizations to ensure access to vaccines. Despite these efforts, the spread of measles remains a concern due to the state's limited infrastructure and growing vaccine skepticism.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Raquel Natalicchio/Houston Chronicle via Getty Images View image in fullscreen The measles outbreak has hit the state’s Amish community, which has low vaccination rates, particularly hard. Photograph: Raquel Natalicchio/Houston Chronicle via Getty Images Pennsylvania health officials brace for expected spread of measles outbreak Most counties don’t have their own health agencies, and some specific population groups have low vaccination rates Pennsylvania is bracing for a wave of measles cases, with some public health officials concerned that it’s “only a matter of time” until the disease spreads beyond the outbreak’s center. Last week the state reported its fifth measles-associated death amid 943 confirmed cases across 39 counties. The outbreak comes amid concerns that public health in the US has been hit by increasing vaccine skepticism, especially due to the power and influence of the controversial health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr. Contributing to the challenge is that Pennsylvania is one of the few US states where its counties mostly lack their own health department, with only seven of its 67 counties having their own. Pennsylvania reports fifth measles-related death of the year amid outbreak Read more Lancaster county, which doesn’t have its own health department, has seen the highest number of cases, at 381, believed to be mostly among the Amish or similar religious groups who have historically low rates of measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine uptake. Health officials, doctors and other members of the community in the county are now attempting to establish its own health department to better tackle the outbreak. Jeanne Franklin, the director of Chester county’s health department, said that her county, which has the third highest number of cases, at 83, is preparing for an influx of cases from neighboring Lancaster. “We have a very long border with Lancaster county, and these types of disease don’t care about borders,” Franklin said. “It’s going to keep going and find a person who isn’t vaccinated. We’re operating with the assumption that the disease may jump and spread to non-Plain [Amish] community members. Our goal is to prevent that, but we assume that it may happen.” She added that although many Amish people have not been vaccinated, it’s not because they are anti-vaxxers, but rather because “they were either unaware of it or they don’t seek out healthcare as frequently as other residents”. She added: “But they’re willing to have the conversation and make a decision on what’s happening today. They’re not making decisions based on the myths out there, but because their neighbors are impacted.” Amid active surveillance of measles cases, contact tracing and setting up community vaccination centers, the county is also working to dispel misinformation some of its residents have regarding the safety of the vaccine. “We have people in key positions saying it isn’t an outbreak, it isn’t a problem. We have people who approach our nurses and say that they were told the vaccine will make them sterile, or that there’s a microchip within it,” Franklin said. But for Franklin, the fact that Chester county is one of the few counties to have its own health department is a clear advantage in the race to stop the spread of the disease. “By having our own health department [it] means that we already have the infrastructure in place, and we prepare for these things all year round,” Franklin said. “With the number of counties [the state has] to provide support to, they can’t possibly know all the key partners in a geographical area.” She added: “The state has a finite set of employees, while I can pull my employees away from their normal jobs and say we need help with this. We live here, we know it, and we’re working with the communities and the organizations who know us. I think every county needs a health department, but we’re just not set up that way in Pennsylvania while other states are.” Dr Richard Lorraine, the medical director of Montgomery county’s office of public health, said that it was a “matter of time” before the third most populous county in Pennsylvania would begin to see measles cases. “We recognize that essentially it’s all around us, and so a big focus for the county is immunization,” Lorraine said. “It’s the best preventative measure and where the county is focusing its efforts on.” skip past newsletter promotion after newsletter promotion Working closely with community organizers, Lorraine said the county was making sure that every resident was able to be vaccinated regardless of their insurance status. “Because we are a public health agency, our mandate is to provide for members of our population for the most part who are underinsured, and there’s a very high percentage who aren’t insured,” Lorraine said. “And through our partnership with pharmacies we’re able to open these clinics for everyone. So whether you have insurance or if you don’t, we can work it out so that someone is able to administer a vaccine.” Lora Werner, the director of the Delaware county health department, said that her agency was facing several challenges in protecting residents from the virus. Among them was the collapse of Crozer Health, Delaware county’s largest healthcare system, due to bankruptcy last year. “They were a huge provider of pediatric care,” Werner said. “And although we do have a high vaccination rate across Delaware county, there are pockets where they aren’t as good.” For Werner, the newly established local health department has been a gamechanger for promoting public health initiatives in the county. “We are the newest health department on the block, and we’re so passionate about the fact that we’re now here and can serve our residents,” Werner said. “We’re deeply integrated into the social networks.” She added: “Creating a new department was very challenging, and we’re a very odd state in this regard,” Werner said. “If you want to have one you need to have a referendum to establish one. But it’s not required, so we have a mishmash of the counties that have taken that step and those that have not.” The Guardian has approached the Pennsylvania health department for comment. Pa. health officials face multiple fronts as measles outbreak overlaps with respiratory virus season

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pa. health officials face multiple fronts as measles outbreak overlaps with respiratory virus season

News Brief
Pennsylvania is facing a significant measles outbreak with 1,078 cases and 5 deaths, as the state health officials respond to the outbreak and the start of the respiratory virus season. Vaccination efforts have increased, with nearly double the number of MMR vaccines administered since the outbreak began. The state is coordinating with federal health agencies for support, but the CDC has not publicly recognized all associated deaths. The outbreak has placed Pennsylvania at odds with the U.S. health secretary, who led the CDC.
Original source text (English)
Vials of measles, mumps and rubella vaccine are displayed on a counter at a Walgreens Pharmacy in Mill Valley, California, in a 2015 file photo. (Photo illustration by Justin Sullivan/Getty Images) This story was originally published by WESA . It is not available for republishing. Pennsylvania's measles outbreak crossed a historic threshold this week: at 1,078 cases in 41 counties, the state has recorded the nation's largest measles outbreak in the last three decades. Five people have died with infections and nearly 200 people have been hospitalized. As the outbreak overlaps with the start of the respiratory virus season, state health officials are facing several fronts. And despite a recent rift with the Trump administration , it appears that federal help is still on the table. "It's all hands on deck," Pennsylvania Health Secretary Dr. Debra Bogen said Monday. "We have a very robust response from our own Department of Health as well as many of our partners across the state." Local officials confirmed the first case among Allegheny County residents Monday , though people were exposed in August at Children's Hospital of Pittsburgh when infected patients from out of town visited the emergency room. Health providers across Pennsylvania have administered nearly double the number of measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccines in a typical month since the outbreak began. Pennsylvania medical providers typically administer around 25,000 doses of the MMR vaccine per month. In August, that number jumped to 49,000 doses. In September, providers administered 53,000 doses of the vaccine. UPMC Presbyterian Hospital in the Oakland neighborhood of Pittsburgh on October 13, 2025. (Photo by Jessica Kourkounis for the Pennsylvania Capital-Star) The MMR vaccine is extremely effective in preventing measles infections, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics. One dose is about 93% effective against infections. The recommended second dose brings that immunity up to 97%, and it's lifelong. Bogen stressed the importance of the MMR vaccine at an event at the Hilltop Pharmacy in Pittsburgh's Allentown neighborhood Monday. "The MMR vaccine has proven to be effective for decades, and we didn't have cases for decades because of the effectiveness of the vaccine," Bogen said. Still, measles infections continue to climb in Pennsylvania. More than half of the state's 67 counties have tallied cases. Nearly 30 new infections were reported just over the weekend. It's not yet clear if the state's effort to ramp up vaccinations have improved waning immunity in Pennsylvania classrooms. One in three kindergarteners in Allegheny County attend class at a school with immunization rates below the recommended threshold. Bogen said the state is coordinating its measles response with federal health agencies such as the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But the outbreak has placed Pennsylvania at odds with U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr . Under his leadership, the CDC website reflects just two of the five measles-associated deaths in Pennsylvania. In a letter to the CDC last month, Bogen requested support from the federal government, but on the condition that the CDC acknowledge the additional deaths. "The CDC's choice not to publicly recognize the deaths undermines our response," Bogen wrote . "If CDC is unable to transparently and accurately communicate information about those measles-associated deaths, the Department will respectfully rescind our request," for aid, she wrote. It's all hands on deck. We have a very robust response from our own Department of Health as well as many of our partners across the state. – Pennsylvania Health Secretary Dr. Debra Bogen On Monday, Bogen doubled down on her comments, contending that "not recognizing the deaths minimizes... the loss of life that we've had to vaccine-preventable illness." "I just want people to understand how serious this infection can be," she said. Bogen said the state is determining the need for additional support from the federal government to manage the measles outbreak. She downplayed the impact of such help, noting that it would add one or two people to the department's staff of 300 already responding to the outbreak. "[We] would welcome any support that comes our way, but again, we do have a very robust response already with very good technical assistance," she said. Bogen joined Allegheny County Health Department officials to highlight the availability of the COVID-19 vaccine ahead of the respiratory virus season. Bogen and Department of Human Services Secretary Val Arkoosh each got updated COVID-19 boosters at the Hilltop Pharmacy. Medical experts broadly recommend the COVID-19 vaccine for everyone aged 6 months and older in consultation with a healthcare provider. Vaccination is especially important for older adults, the immunocompromised and others at higher risk of severe illness, according to the American Medical Association . Pennsylvanians with private insurance can get their COVID-19 and flu vaccines covered with zero out-of-pocket costs, Bogen said. Those without can still get a free shot at federally qualified health centers, state health centers or through the Allegheny County Health Department. Bogen also stressed the importance of the flu vaccine, which she said she planned to get in the coming weeks. Most patients can get the flu and COVID-19 vaccine in the same visit. Forecasting the severity of the upcoming respiratory virus season — when influenza, RSV and COVID-19 are more likely to circulate — is challenging. Health experts often can't determine the dominant flu strain until the season develops. Last year, more than 13,000 adults and 1,200 children were hospitalized with COVID-19 infections in Pennsylvania, according to the health department. Nearly 600 people died. The flu, COVID-19 and measles can all exhibit similar symptoms such as fever, cough and a runny nose. Bogen encouraged people to take a test to determine what kind of infection they have before coming into contact with others, especially the immunocompromised. "For 30 years I didn't ask families who came to my clinic during [the] respiratory [virus] season, 'Have you been exposed to measles?' But nowadays I think that's an additional question people should be asking," Bogen said. 最新的 ODH 更新显示,俄亥俄州 麻疹 病例较去年增加 400%

Ohio measles cases increase 400% from last year in latest ODH update
新闻简报俄亥俄州的 麻疹 麻疹 在 2026 年急剧增加,截至 10 月初,俄亥俄州卫生部在 21 个县确认了 228 例病例,与去年全年报告的 45 例相比增加了 400% 以上。最新的每周数据显示新增 22 例感染病例,较前一周增加 120%,凸显传播加速。大多数患者未接种疫苗,反映出对该州 MMR 覆盖率下降的更广泛担忧。ODH 官员已发出对 疫苗接种 的紧急呼吁,确定了潜在的暴露地点,包括紧急护理诊所和牲畜拍卖场,并正在积极跟踪 暴发。目前情况仍在调查中,公共卫生信息强调,免疫是预防进一步 传播 的最有效保护措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告于2026-10-08发布。俄亥俄州卫生部(ODH)仪表盘在2026年10月更新,显示了当前年度的最新病例数。
地点与对象
麻疹 疫情发生在美国俄亥俄州,横跨 21 个县,2026 年迄今累计报告确诊病例 228 例。
事件
ODH 报告称,俄亥俄州 2026 年 麻疹 传播 病例已增至 21 个县的 228 例,较 2025 年全年记录的 45 例大幅增加。这一增长包括最近一周新增 22 例,表明 传播 病例迅速加速。
规模、影响与风险
病例数比上一年增加了 400%(2025 年为 45 例)。最近的每周数据显示,新增病例从 10 例增加到 22 例,增幅达 120%。卫生官员指出,绝大多数病例是未接种疫苗的个体,这凸显了由于 疫苗接种 覆盖率下降而导致的社区风险增加。
应对、状态与不确定性
ODH 官员敦促将 疫苗接种 作为主要预防措施,并公开强调全面免疫的重要性。该部门继续监测病例地点,调查紧急护理中心和牲畜拍卖等暴露地点,并建议公众寻求 疫苗接种。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio measles cases increase 400% from last year in latest ODH update

News Brief
Ohio’s measles outbreak has surged dramatically in 2026, with the Ohio Department of Health confirming 228 cases across 21 counties as of early October—more than a 400% rise compared with the 45 cases reported for the entire previous year. The latest weekly data show 22 new infections, a 120% increase over the prior week, underscoring accelerating spread. Most patients are unvaccinated, reflecting broader concerns about declining MMR coverage in the state. ODH officials have issued urgent calls for vaccination, identified potential exposure sites including urgent‐care clinics and livestock auctions, and are actively tracking the outbreak. The situation remains under investigation, and public health messaging stresses that immunization is the most effective protection against further transmission.
Original source text (English)
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials Blueprint WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services NEWS Measles Add Topic Ohio measles cases increase 400% from last year in latest ODH update Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Oct. 8, 2026, 2:44 p.m. ET Hear this story Ohio's 2026 measles outbreak has grown to 228 cases across 21 counties, with a significant increase in recent weeks. Ohio's total number of 2026 measles cases is already more than 400% greater than all of 2025. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, surpassing 4,000 infections for the year. Health officials state the vast majority of cases are among unvaccinated individuals and urge vaccination as the best protection. Measles has spread to another Ohio county and another with a near double-digit increase as Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues. The state had 22 new cases in the past week, a 120% increase from the 10 new cases reported the week before, the Ohio Department of Health reports. ODH is reporting 228 cases in 21 counties so far this year, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Oct. 8. That number is 406% greater than the 45 cases reported in all of 2025. Total cases in Ohio this year have continued climbing in recent weeks: 206 cases in 20 counties on Oct. 1 196 cases in 16 counties on Sept. 24 180 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 17 133 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 10 122 cases in 14 counties on Sept. 3 107 cases in 12 counties on Aug. 27 99 cases in 12 counties Aug. 20 69 cases in 11 counties on Aug. 13 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6. Hancock County is the latest in Ohio to record a new measles cases. Additionally, five cases were attributed to unknown counties for the first time. This a week after Ashland, Stark, Vinton and Wood counties all joined the list. The vast majority of cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, per ODH. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Measles cases nationwide have hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. Data released Oct. 7 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 4,020 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991. Measles growing in Ohio; counties reporting cases in 2026 Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashland: 1 Ashtabula: 41 (nine new cases) Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 7 Gallia: 3 Geauga: 50 (one new case) Hancock: 1 (one new case, first time on list) Harrison: 13 (two new cases) Holmes: 9 Knox: 5 Meigs: 4 Morgan: 3 Morrow: 12 (three new cases) Ottawa: 1 Portage: 1 Stark: 1 Trumbull: 8 Tuscarawas: 13 Vinton: 1 Wayne: 45 (one new case) Wood: 1 Unknown county: 5 (five new cases) The case count for the year reflects previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with one case in 2023, seven in 2024 and 45 cases in 2025. Pennsylvania reports measles deaths At least five people this year have died from measles in Pennsylvania as of Sept. 30, 2026, according to USA TODAY. None of the individuals were vaccinated, USA TODAY reports. The first two measles deaths were the first in Pennsylvania in 35 years, the state Department of Health said in an Aug. 25 news release, and the first such deaths in the United States this year, according to Centers for Disease Control and Prevention data. Where are measles cases spreading in Ohio? Portage County confirmed its first measles case, the county Health District announced Sept. 8. At the time, no public exposure locations associated with this case had been identified. Harrison County Public Health reported one case of measles as of Aug. 7, but the state dashboard shows additional cases there. Hancock County Public Health reported one measles case in Findlay with possible exposure to the public at NWO Urgent Care on September 29 from 2:30–5:30 p.m. and Blanchard Valley Hospital Emergency Department the same day from 3:30–6:30 p.m. Geauga Public Health reports that the individuals with measles visited several locations while able to spread the virus, including Bloomfield Horse Sale, 2211 Kinsman Road NW, on July 18. The Tuscarawas health department reports that another of its cases was exposed at the Bloomfield Livestock Auction, at the same address as Bloomfield Horse Sale, on on the same day. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Measles cases on the rise as Ohio vaccination rates fluctuate in state, U.S. Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Ohio's vaccination rate for the 2025-26 school year slipped well below the herd immunity threshold, making it susceptible to outbreaks. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventive measures, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine. Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump in August 2026 signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, according to USA TODAY. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lowers overall vaccination rates, according to experts. The MMR vaccine has been in existence since 1971, according to the World Health Organization. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Featured Weekly Ad

意见:麻疹 和死亡。CDC 的否认主义 - Hartford Courant

Opinion: Measles and death. Denialism at the CDC - Hartford Courant
新闻简报文章讨论了 CDC 在统计 麻疹 死亡人数方面的变化,这导致与宾夕法尼亚州报告的 2026 年 4 例 麻疹 相关死亡人数存在差异。CDC 现在使用来自国家卫生统计中心的数据,该系统专注于 死亡率 趋势,而不是州和地方卫生官员的报告。这一转变引发了人们对公共卫生数据准确性的担忧,因为 CDC 目前的计数为零,而 2026 年的 麻疹 病例已经超过 3,300 例,标志着美国几十年来 麻疹 病例最糟糕的一年。这一变化归因于国务卿小罗伯特·F·肯尼迪 (Robert F. Kennedy,Jr.),他因在 疫苗 安全性和 麻疹 严重性问题上的立场而受到批评。文章强调了此类政策变化对公共卫生信任的潜在影响以及准确数据报告的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于 2026 年 10 月 7 日。2026 年的 麻疹 麻疹 病例已超过 3,300 例,宾夕法尼亚州卫生官员报告 2026 年有 4 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。
地点与对象
麻疹 正在美国发生,特别是在宾夕法尼亚州,据报道有四例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。CDC 是参与追踪和报告 麻疹 死亡事件的联邦机构。
事件
疾病预防控制中心改变了计算 麻疹 死亡人数的方法,使用国家卫生统计中心的数据,而不是州和地方卫生官员的报告。这一变化导致疾病预防控制中心的官方统计数据与宾夕法尼亚州报告的死亡人数之间存在差异。
规模、影响与风险
CDC目前统计麻疹死亡人数为零,而宾夕法尼亚州卫生官员已确定2026年有四起与麻疹相关的死亡。2026年麻疹 麻疹病例已超过3,300例,成为美国麻疹最糟糕的一年。统计方法的改变引发了人们对公共卫生数据准确性的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心改变了计算 麻疹 死亡人数的方法,这使得州当局报告的死亡人数不包括在联邦统计中。文章认为,这一变化可能是为了尽量减少报告的死亡率。CDC 的新方法基于国家卫生统计中心的数据,该数据旨在跟踪 死亡率 趋势,而不是确定个人死亡情况。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Opinion: Measles and death. Denialism at the CDC - Hartford Courant

News Brief
The article discusses the CDC's change in how it counts measles deaths, which has led to a discrepancy with Pennsylvania's reported four measles-associated deaths in 2026. The CDC now uses data from the National Center for Health Statistics, a system focused on mortality trends, rather than state and local health officials' reports. This shift has raised concerns about the accuracy of public health data, as the CDC's current count is zero, while the outbreak in 2026 has already surpassed 3,300 cases, marking it as the worst year for measles in the U.S. in decades. The change is attributed to Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., who has been criticized for his stance on vaccine safety and measles severity. The article highlights the potential impact of such policy changes on public health trust and the importance of accurate data reporting.
Original source text (English)
FILE – This undated image made available by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention on Feb. 4, 2015, shows an electron microscope image of a measles virus particle. (Cynthia Goldsmith/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, via AP, File) By Seth C. Kalichman | Professor of Psychological Sciences at UConn PUBLISHED: October 7, 2026 at 5:27 AM EDT Getting your Trinity Audio player ready... In the midst of the worst U.S. measles outbreak in decades, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has abruptly changed how it counts deaths from measles. Pennsylvania health officials have identified four measles-associated deaths in 2026 . The CDC currently counts zero. The discrepancy is not because Pennsylvania and the CDC disagree about whether people died. It is about what counts as a measles death. It is about the CDC , under the direction of Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., denying that measles is as big a problem as it is. The CDC now counts measles deaths using data from the National Center for Health Statistics, a system designed primarily for tracking mortality trends rather than identifying individual deaths. Previously, measles deaths were counted using reports from state and local health officials. The change at the CDC has left deaths officially recognized by state authorities out of the federal tally. The CDC changed its approach to counting measles deaths after Secretary Kennedy refuted the deaths reported by Pennsylvania health officials. The changes made at the CDC minimized their reported death rate. Changing the definition of measles deaths occurred as the evidence for an expanding measles epidemic became inconvenient for Secretary Kennedy. The changed definition essentially moved the goalposts. Moving the goalposts is one of the more familiar tactics of denialism. Whether denying climate change, HIV causes AIDS, the severity of COVID-19, or the effectiveness of vaccines, denialists repeatedly change what counts as evidence when the evidence contradicts their beliefs. Denialism is not new to public policy. We have seen policymakers refute scientific evidence on climate change, vaccine safety, and HIV/AIDS. That brings us to back to Secretary Kennedy. What distinguishes Mr. Kennedy is the breadth of scientific facts he has challenged over the years, from refuting the safety and effectiveness of vaccines to supporting claims that HIV does not cause AIDS. Many of the alarms sounded about his record during his Senate confirmation hearings are now being realized. Last year, Mr. Kennedy dismissed all 17 members of the CDC’s Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices and replaced them with a smaller panel of mostly vaccine skeptics and denialists. The new committee subsequently changed federal vaccine policy. Routine hepatitis B vaccination at birth is no longer recommended for infants whose mothers test negative for hepatitis B. COVID-19 vaccination recommendations shifted toward individual decision-making. Despite no evidence that thimerosal is harmful, it is now federal policy to recommend thimerosal-free influenza vaccines. The CDC also reorganized the childhood immunization schedule, reducing the number of vaccines universally recommended for children. These were not the only changes. Mr. Kennedy also terminated promising mRNA vaccine research. He has repeatedly emphasized vitamin A in discussions of measles prevention and treatment. While vitamin A has a role in treating severe measles, it does not prevent measles. Discussing vitamins in the context of vaccination creates yet more confusion. And during all of this chaos, measles continues to spread. Johns Hopkins University’s U.S. measles tracker reported 2,289 cases in 2025. The number in 2026 has already surpassed 3,300, making this the worst year for measles in the United States in decades. And regardless of what the CDC is counting, people are dying of measles. CT vaccine opponents shout down legislators and health officials at Capitol event On September 17, Mr. Kennedy addressed the convention of the Children’s Health Defense, the anti-vaccination-focused organization that he founded and formerly chaired. Secretary Kennedy told the audience “Let me be clear. You have a strong and steadfast friend at the White House.” He also said, “In our country we care about rare illnesses and deaths. We care about every child who dies from measles. They get headlines. I’d ask you to care about children who are dying or suffering or being injured by vaccine injuries.” Pennsylvania state health authorities report four measles-associated deaths, so far, this year. The CDC says there are none. It is reasonable for the CDC to refine how medical records are examined and how laboratory procedures are conducted. But it is wrong for the CDC to make inconvenient truths disappear by changing how and what events are counted. Public health serves us best when we can trust the CDC is counting what happened, not deciding to count what Secretary Kennedy prefers to have happened. Seth C. Kalichman is a professor of Psychological Sciences at UConn , Storrs and a public health researcher. His work focuses on infectious diseases. The article was published on October 7, 2026. The measles outbreak in 2026 has already surpassed 3,300 cases, and Pennsylvania health officials reported four measles-associated deaths in 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in the United States, specifically in Pennsylvania, where four measles-associated deaths were reported. The CDC is the federal agency involved in tracking and reporting measles deaths.
Event
The CDC changed its method of counting measles deaths, using data from the National Center for Health Statistics instead of state and local health officials' reports. This change has led to a discrepancy between the CDC's official count and Pennsylvania's reported deaths.
Scale, impact and risk
The CDC currently counts zero measles deaths, while Pennsylvania health officials have identified four measles-associated deaths in 2026. The measles outbreak in 2026 has already surpassed 3,300 cases, making it the worst year for measles in the U.S. The change in counting methods has raised concerns about the accuracy of public health data.
Response, status and uncertainty

The article suggests that this change may be an attempt to minimize the reported death rate. The CDC's new approach is based on data from the National Center for Health Statistics, which is designed for tracking mortality trends rather than identifying individual deaths.

Measles Outbreak, Vaccine Information, and Public Health with Dr. Lori Handy from CHOP - 93.3 WMMR Philadelphia

Measles Outbreak, Vaccine Information, and Public Health with Dr. Lori Handy from CHOP - 93.3 WMMR Philadelphia

News Brief
The article, published on October 7, 2026, by 93.3 WMMR Philadelphia, features Dr. Lori Handy, an infectious disease physician at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia (CHOP), discussing the importance and safety of vaccines, particularly the measles vaccine. The piece highlights the resurgence of measles and its potential complications, such as pneumonia, dehydration, and encephalitis, as well as rare but severe outcomes like acute demyelinating encephalomyelitis (ADEM) and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE). It notes that one of the four measles-related deaths in Pennsylvania in 2026 was attributed to ADEM, which occurred in an 18-year-old boy. The article does not report a confirmed outbreak or specific cases but emphasizes the need for up-to-date MMR vaccination to prevent measles and its associated risks. It serves as an educational piece rather than a detailed outbreak report, focusing on public health awareness and vaccine safety.
Original source text (English)
Human Interest Measles Outbreak, Vaccine Information, and Public Health with Dr. Lori Handy from CHOP Dr. Lori Handy, The Associate Director of the Vaccine Education Center and a practicing Physician in infectious diseases at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia, was on the Public Affairs Show to... Markus Goldman Updated Oct 7, 2026 3:39 PM EDT Dr. Lori Handy, The Associate Director of the Vaccine Education Center and a practicing Physician in infectious diseases at Children's Hospital of Philadelphia , was on the Public Affairs Show to discuss the importance and safety of vaccines. Dr. Handy dispels the terrible misinformation and lies about vaccines by discussing the science of a vaccine. We also talk about how it reacts with the human body both positively and negatively. Dr. Handy discusses how much vaccines have been studied, and how they have been studied. Dr. Handy also describes what it's like to get sick from measles, pertussis, chicken pox and a few other diseases that can be prevented by vaccines. You can get more information about vaccines at The Children's Hospital of Philadelphia's Vaccine Education Center, here . Measles Information : Dr. Paul Offit outlines three common causes of hospitalization and death from measles: pneumonia, dehydration and encephalitis. He then details three lesser-known severe outcomes of measles. The first is immune amnesia, a condition that depletes memory B and T cells. This increases a person’s susceptibility to other infections to which they were previously immune. The second is acute demyelinating encephalomyelitis (ADEM), a rare but often severe autoimmune disorder. As Dr. Offit describes, one of the four deaths in Pennsylvania in 2026 was because of ADEM. It occurred in an 18-year-old boy. The third condition he describes is subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a delayed, uniformly fatal chronic brain infection. SSPE occurs when the virus continues to replicate in the person’s brain after they appear to have recovered. Years after the initial measles infection, the person begins to lose personality features and motor skills until they succumb to the infection. With the resurgence of measles, Dr. Offit reiterates the importance of staying up to date on MMR vaccination. Weaken the virus: Viruses are weakened, so they reproduce very poorly once inside the body. Vaccines for measles, mumps, rubella , rotavirus , polio (oral version; not used in the U.S.), chickenpox (varicella) , and influenza (intranasal version) are made this way. These are all vaccines that protect against viruses. Viruses cannot reproduce on their own. They need cells from other organisms to make more virus particles. Viruses reproduce thousands of times during a natural infection, meaning an infection that occurs after exposure to the virus in the community. Live, weakened vaccine viruses usually reproduce fewer than 20 times. This is enough to cause immunity, but not enough to cause disease. The immune response generates "memory B cells" that protect against infection in the future. Find out more about these and other cells of the immune system. Thank you for listening. The advantage of live, weakened vaccines is that one or two doses usually lead to lifelong immunity. The limitation of this approach is that these kinds of vaccines usually cannot be given to people with weakened immune systems (like people with cancer or AIDS). Find out more about what happens when the immune system doesn’t work properly. Markus is also the WMMR Public Affairs Director. The Beasley Public Affairs Show airs at 6:30AM every Sunday Morning on WMMR. Markus has been at WMMR for almost 22 years. 在数十年来最糟糕的 麻疹 暴发 中,一家诊所依靠多年来来之不易的信任来确保弱势群体的安全 - CNN

Amid worst measles outbreak in decades, one clinic leans on years of hard-won trust to keep vulnerable people safe - CNN
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州中部,特别是米夫林县的 麻疹 暴发 已达到数十年来的最差水平。自 2026 年 4 月以来,已报告了 1,000 多例病例,超过了德克萨斯州和南卡罗来纳州最近的 暴发。麻疹 已导致大量住院患者和 5 人死亡,有关 麻疹 相关死亡的定义和公众传播存在争议。宾夕法尼亚州中部诊所以较低的 疫苗接种 比率为脆弱的阿米什人和平原社区提供服务,正在努力维护信任和安全。尽管麻疹 治疗缺乏先进的设施,诊所还是实施了分诊措施,并强调了疫苗接种的重要性。该诊所的工作人员,包括凯瑟琳·马西亚博士,一直致力于提供准确的信息并确保向社区传达一致的信息。暴发 测试了诊所与其患者和更广泛社区的关系,强调了 疫苗接种 吸收有限地区的公共卫生应对挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 自 2026 年 4 月以来一直在进行,文章于 2026 年 10 月 7 日发表。事件日期没有明确说明,但 暴发 被描述为截至 2026 年 10 月 2 日星期三,美国几十年来最大、最致命的事件。
地点与对象
暴发 以宾夕法尼亚州米夫林县为中心,特别是贝尔维尔,该镇以阿米什人和平原社区而闻名。这些社区的 麻疹 比率较低,并且由于其遗传性疾病而特别容易受到 麻疹 的影响。
事件
麻疹 麻疹 暴发 正在宾夕法尼亚州中部发生,自 2026 年 4 月以来报告了 1,000 多例病例。麻疹 被描述为美国几十年来最严重的一次,它导致大量入院和死亡,特别是在弱势群体中。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 10 月 2 日,宾夕法尼亚州已报告超过 1,000 起 麻疹 病例,超过了德克萨斯州和南卡罗来纳州最近的 麻疹 病例。暴发 已导致五人死亡,死因成为公众争论的话题。该诊所为弱势群体服务,麻疹 造成了前所未有的住院人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
宾夕法尼亚州中部诊所正在积极响应 暴发,与社区保持沟通、提供准确信息并实施分诊措施。然而,该诊所没有能力治疗麻疹患者,而麻疹引发了有关麻疹相关死亡的定义以及如何与公众共享信息的争议。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Amid worst measles outbreak in decades, one clinic leans on years of hard-won trust to keep vulnerable people safe - CNN

News Brief
A measles outbreak in central Pennsylvania, particularly in Mifflin County, has reached its worst levels in decades. Over 1,000 cases have been reported since April 2026, surpassing recent outbreaks in Texas and South Carolina. The outbreak has caused significant hospital admissions and five deaths, with debates over the definition of measles-related deaths and public communication. The Central Pennsylvania Clinic, which serves vulnerable Amish and Plain communities with lower vaccination rates, is working to maintain trust and safety. Despite the lack of advanced facilities for measles treatment, the clinic has implemented triage measures and emphasized the importance of vaccination. The clinic’s staff, including Dr. Katherine Mascia, has been focused on providing accurate information and ensuring consistent messaging to the community. The outbreak has tested the clinic’s relationships with its patients and the broader community, highlighting the challenges of public health response in areas with limited vaccination uptake.
Original source text (English)
Belleville, Pennsylvania — Main Street is bustling on a Wednesday afternoon in late September. Cars mix with horse-and-buggy carriages on the road, many coming and going from the weekly Sale Day — a market spread over 10 acres with livestock, fruits and vegetables, flowers and other goods available to purchase. Lively conversations in English and Pennsylvania Dutch fill the barn where a produce auction stretches until lunchtime; bids for a pint of bright red cherry tomatoes run from 90 cents to $1.30. As dozens of people mix and mingle through the afternoon — Amish and other Plain people, so named for their simple way of dressing, as well as “English,” as they refer to folks outside of their community — there are no direct indications that a measles outbreak is raging in the area. Only one woman has a mask on, which she says she’s been wearing any time she’s left home since the start of the Covid-19 pandemic. One vendor is closed for “unexpected family obligations,” and one regular auction attendee is mourned after her recent sudden death after having “flu-like symptoms.” Tourists are visiting from Brooklyn. But less than a mile away at the Central Pennsylvania Clinic, measles is front and center. Ad Feedback A turn off of Main Street quickly opens up to an idyllic rural valley, filled with lush farms set to a backdrop of rolling green mountains. An unlined road leads across a babbling river to reveal a striking gray building up on a hill, a massive barn raised in the traditional style that uses only wood pegs — no nails — to hold the structure together. The front door is locked, with a photo and description of measles hanging in the window, along with a notice asking visitors to “ring doorbell for service.” Dr. Katherine Mascia swings the door open with a big smile and a quick question: “Any measles symptoms today?” Beneath the genuine smile is true heartbreak. A massive measles outbreak in the area — as of Wednesday, the largest and deadliest the United States has had in decades — is bringing significant pain and suffering to the families she cares for. “In the five years that I’ve worked here, I don’t think we’ve had so many patients admitted to the hospital all within the span of a few weeks,” Mascia said. “We might usually have maybe one patient who’s admitted for their complex care needs, but to have so many all at the same time, it’s really unprecedented.” The Central Pennsylvania Clinic is centered around caring for children and adults with genetic disorders that are especially common among the Amish and broader Plain community, a group that also tends to have lower vaccination rates than average. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Defying politics More than 1,000 measles cases have been reported by the Pennsylvania Department of Health since the outbreak started in April, surpassing recent record-breaking outbreaks – in Texas and South Carolina last year – to become the worst in the US since the early 1990s. The clinic’s home of Mifflin County is one of the hardest-hit, near the epicenter in nearby Lancaster County. The patients who the Central Pennsylvania Clinic serves are especially vulnerable to the severe effects of a measles infection. The nonprofit is centered around caring for children and adults with genetic disorders that are especially common among the Amish and broader Plain community, a group that also tends to have lower vaccination rates than average. The Plain people are known for their ​caution toward government and other nonreligious authority. But as measles tears through many of their hometowns in central Pennsylvania, the national conversation has become paradoxically politically charged. Leaders from the federal government and the Pennsylvania government have clashed over the response , with much of the debate centered around the five deaths that have come during this outbreak. Disputes over the definition of what counts as a measles death and how news of the deaths is shared with the public have disrupted public health response plans. The strength of the trust between the clinic and its community has faced some of its greatest tests over the past few months. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Meanwhile, the Central Pennsylvania Clinic’s staff is focused on how to best keep everyone safe and healthy. They’re leaning on the clinic’s well-forged foundation of trust. It has taken persistence and patience — sometimes years — for the clinic to build those relationships, and the strength of that trust has faced some of its greatest tests over the past few months. When Pennsylvania reported a measles-associated death in an 18-year-old in Mifflin County, Mascia said, the clinic started to get tons of calls — from patients they knew and other members of the broader community — who just wanted to know what happened. They weren’t paying attention to what government officials were arguing about on social media, she said. They wanted a clear understanding of how the young person died. “I’m quite honored that they’re calling us and asking those questions. It helps me feel like they really trust us,” Mascia said. “But it puts pressure on us, too.” She said she reached out to the county coroner directly to get a formal statement on the cause of death, and she references that statement when patients have questions. “We want to keep that line of communication open with the community, and it’s so important to provide accurate information and be consistent with the messaging,” she said. Morning light is seen over corn fields and farmland in Belleville, Pennsylvania on Friday, October 2, 2026. Mifflin County, where Belleville is situated, is near the epicenter of the largest measles outbreak in the United States. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Although the clinic is well-equipped in many ways, it's not built to treat measles patients. Michelle Gustafson for CNN The clinic has uniquely carved out space for opportunities to build a rapport with the community. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Consistent communication and a resilient response Central Pennsylvania Clinic leaders have been thinking about and planning for a measles outbreak in the community since the start of the West Texas outbreak in January 2025, Mascia said. Their patients are vulnerable, and their offices, while incredibly well-equipped in many ways, are not built to treat measles patients. “We don’t have advanced ventilation systems, and we don’t have negative-pressure rooms, so if a person who is infected with measles comes in, our entire building is compromised for two hours,” Mascia said. Pennsylvania requires a throat swab, a blood sample and a urine sample to confirm a measles case — arduous tasks for both ill patients, who often had to take a taxing horse and buggy ride to the clinic, and for clinic staff who have to work within the constraints of their facility. The rear entrance to the clinic building, which also hosts a federally qualified health center, has become a makeshift triage center for people with suspected measles cases. The back parking lot is large enough for cars and buggies to spread out, and clinic staffers meet families at the door, where they can lead the patient into a small storage barn to collect urine samples. It was important to have a logistical plan to keep operations safe, Mascia said, but she pointedly explains that the most critical way to protect their patients is to raise vaccination rates. The measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) vaccine is extremely effective. One dose is 93% effective against measles, and the recommended second dose bumps that protection up to 97% — and the immunity is lifelong. Extensive research has also demonstrated the vaccine’s safety . Childhood vaccination rates have been falling nationwide, with a record share of parents opting to exempt their new kindergartners from the required shots last school year. Vaccination rates tend to be lower among Plain people, but it’s not because of an inherent opposition, experts say. There are certain shared values within the community, but there are also varied individual beliefs and opinions about vaccines, as there would in any broad group of people, Mascia said. Consistency in the clinic’s messaging around vaccination has been the key to raising coverage rates. If the patients sense any type of coercion from doctors or if they feel forced into a decision about something like a vaccination, they’ll completely disengage from all health care – an outcome that would be particularly troublesome for the vulnerable patients they care for. “We share that it’s our strong recommendation that everyone be fully vaccinated,” Mascia said. “And if they’re not interested, we repeat our recommendation and say ‘we’re here to help if and when you change your mind.’” In the Amish and broader Plain community, the experience of a neighbor and advice from a family member carry significantly more weight than findings from a study published in the most prestigious medical journal or recommendations from the leading clinical society, said Dr. Helge Hartung, who joined the Central Pennsylvania Clinic last year. The Central Pennsylvania Clinic has uniquely carved out space for opportunities to build that kind of rapport – but it takes time. Two hours are blocked off for new patient visits, and every visit after that is scheduled for an hour. The main exam room ​is extra large, with space to fit the whole family — the average of which includes seven children — for conversations that affect every member. There’s time and space to answer questions, Mascia said, and her well-informed patients come with many of them. “What I’m seeing are families trying to weigh all the information that they are getting from us, from the community, from other sources,” she said. “They are families truly trying to make the best decision for their child.” A few years ago, the federal government stopped providing MMR shots to the clinic through Vaccines for Children, a federally funded program that provides routine immunizations at no cost to children who might not otherwise be able to afford them. The government told Mascia that it was withdrawing because of the lack of demand for the shots, she said. But cost is a key consideration for many of her patients when making health care decisions, and she worried about the families who did want to get vaccinated but couldn’t afford it after this change. The state government has since stepped up to help keep the clinic’s small in-house pharmacy sufficiently stocked with MMR vaccines, and the timing has been critical. Mascia says she hasn’t pushed the issue of vaccination much more than usual since the measles outbreak started — just the standard consistent messaging — but the requests from families have noticeably increased. A handmade quilt adorns the clinic's vestibule. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Dr. Helge Hartung leads the clinic's home care program. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Confronting challenges The Pennsylvania Department of Health has hosted nearly 200 pop-up vaccination clinics where workers have given thousands of MMR doses since the start of the measles outbreak this spring. Government-sponsored programs like this would typically deter most Plain people, but the Central Pennsylvania Clinic has built up enough trust in the community that simply hosting vaccination events in the clinic building has been enough to drive significant turnout. More than two dozen people were vaccinated at a pop-up clinic it hosted in mid-August, and 73 more were vaccinated at a second one in mid-September. Interest in the September pop-up spiked significantly after the death in Mifflin County was reported. “That shows me that this community does care about preventing those serious consequences,” Mascia said. “They want to do what they can, and they know that our strongest tool is through vaccinations.” Vaccinating a hundred people within the Belleville community “is considered very successful,” the state health department said. “One of the most important strategies for building trust is recognizing that a successful response must include the people who Pennsylvanians turn to in their daily lives for guidance, like primary care doctors, pharmacists, faith leaders, and community leaders,” the state health department said in an email to CNN. “The Central Pennsylvania Clinic has been an integral part of the Belleville community for more than a decade, caring for the medical needs of these Pennsylvanians and creating lasting relationships. Their partnership in hosting these vaccination clinics provides a layer of trust that DOH can build upon to help residents understand the unmatched protection available through the MMR vaccine.” The Central Pennsylvania Clinic also aims to meet patients where they are — both philosophically and physically. Doctors and nurses regularly make house visits to see patients whose health conditions or financial situations make it difficult to travel to the offices. But patients have increasingly requested home visits because fear of infection during the measles outbreak has made them afraid to leave their homes. Horse-and-buggy carriages share space with cars in Belleville. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Clinic staff is happy to travel to patients’ homes to administer MMR vaccines when requested, too. Sometimes the visits are coordinated to be at a certain hour or to a certain part of the property, to avoid raising eyebrows among neighbors. Hartung, who leads the clinic’s home care program, knows a family who skipped going to church for more than month to avoid possible measles exposures to their vulnerable infant. As soon as the baby turned 6 months, they requested a home visit for an early vaccination. “The first thing the mother said was, ‘Now we can go to church again,’” Hartung said. “If they’re missing church, it goes to show you how significant the fear and concern is.” But the measles outbreak has added even more layers of complication to the home visit operation. Instead of mapping the most efficient route between homes, Hartung is now planning his home visit schedule based on the patients’ vulnerability to an infection. It adds dozens of miles and significantly more time between appointments. “In a normal time, we might do it by distance, a straightforward route. Now we’re really looking at the most vulnerable sequence, just in case there is a measles encounter that we didn’t anticipate,” he said. “We, of course, are all vaccinated, but we always worry that we might actually become a vector, so we plan that out the best we can.” Hartung says he knows that he’s gained a patient’s trust when they start calling him by his last name. When formal titles are dropped, it’s a sign that he has found his way in. “When they’re very formal, it could go either way,” he said. “It’s great to hear, ‘Hartung, what do you think?’” In recent weeks, Dr. Katherine Mascia says, there’s been a marked increase in patients cancelling appointments or simply not showing up to the clinic. Michelle Gustafson for CNN The rear entrance to the clinic building has become a makeshift triage center for people with suspected measles cases. Michelle Gustafson for CNN Wide-reaching worries Mascia recently needed to send a young patient to the emergency department, where the family learned that the closest newborn intensive care unit was full and had to be diverted to another hospital system. There have been only a few other times that patients have been diverted to a different hospital system due to a lack of available beds, Mascia said, but the typically rare event has been happening much more frequently during this measles outbreak. And in recent weeks, Mascia says, there’s been a marked increase in patients cancelling appointments or simply not showing up to the clinic. “Sometimes it’s a sudden, eerie kind of stillness,” she said. “Whenever there’s a missed appointment, I’m always concerned that something really severe happened. Right now, you just have to assume the patient has measles.” The clinic almost always has the time and space to reschedule or fit in a new appointment, but missed appointments aren’t always rescheduled, and gaps in care can lead to serious consequences for her patients. “It’s very important to keep on top of these conditions to make sure patients have the best possible outcomes,” Mascia said. Young children can rapidly outgrow dosages for important medications or miss the chance to manage a condition with physical therapy before needing surgery. Measles has also kept the broader community away from fundraising events that are critical to keeping the nonprofit up and running. Plain families put handmade quilts, baked goods, farm equipment and livestock up for sale and gift their earnings back to the clinic. Michelle Gustafson for CNN The vast majority of the Central Pennsylvania Clinic’s operating budget comes from direct community donations, many of which happen at large auctions that take place a few times throughout the year. Plain families will put handmade quilts, baked goods, farm equipment and sometimes livestock up for sale and gift their earnings back to the clinic. Clinic executive director Louise Yoder said that a few of the most recent auctions were a bit less successful – and measles was one of the main reasons why. “People are sick and not wanting to go to events like this,” she said. Part of the clinic’s goal in providing culturally competent care to the community is an understanding that finances can be restrictive for many patients. The team works with local hospitals to negotiate special rates for their patients who are Amish or members of the broader Plain community, a group whose philosophical beliefs keep them from enrolling in formal health insurance plans. But the measles outbreak is raising the stakes. “With the sheer number of hospital stays and ICU admissions that we’re seeing because of measles, I do wonder about the financial toll on this community,” Mascia said. “It concerns me.”...

The outbreak has been ongoing since April 2026, with the article published on October 7, 2026. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the outbreak is described as the largest and deadliest in the US in decades as of Wednesday, October 2, 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak is centered in Mifflin County, Pennsylvania, particularly in Belleville, a town known for its Amish and Plain communities. These communities have lower vaccination rates and are especially vulnerable to measles due to their genetic disorders.
Event
A measles outbreak is occurring in central Pennsylvania, with over 1,000 cases reported since April 2026. The outbreak is described as the worst in the US in decades, and it has led to significant hospital admissions and deaths, particularly among vulnerable populations.
Scale, impact and risk
As of October 2, 2026, over 1,000 measles cases have been reported in Pennsylvania, surpassing recent outbreaks in Texas and South Carolina. The outbreak has resulted in five deaths, with the cause of death being a subject of public debate. The clinic serves a vulnerable population, and measles has caused unprecedented hospital admissions.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Central Pennsylvania Clinic is actively responding to the outbreak by maintaining communication with the community, providing accurate information, and implementing triage measures. However, the clinic is not equipped to treat measles patients, and the outbreak has led to disputes over the definition of measles-related deaths and how information is shared with the public.

宾夕法尼亚州 麻疹 暴发 病例数突破 1,000 例,这是美国自 1991 年以来最糟糕的一年 - FOX 5 亚特兰大

Pennsylvania measles outbreak passes 1,000 cases in worst year for US since 1991 - FOX 5 Atlanta
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州一起麻疹疫情已超过1000例,标志着该州近三十年来最大的暴发疫情。疫情始于2026年4月,已导致5人死亡和近200例住院。卫生官员将疫情激增归因于低疫苗接种率,这些率已低于防止暴发传播所需的95%阈值。此次暴发疫情使全国总病例数接近3900例,为自1991年以来的最高水平。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)强调该病毒的高传染性,指出暴露于病毒的10名易感人群中,有9人可能感染。国际卫生官员将召开会议,重新评估美国和墨西哥的麻疹无疫状态。该疫情由FOX 5亚特兰大报道,数据来源于美国疾病控制与预防中心和美联社。目前疫情仍在积极调查中,应对措施包括监测和提高疫苗接种率。

据报道,暴发于2026年4月开始,最新更新于2026年10月5日。发布日期为2026年10月6日。

暴发 发生在宾夕法尼亚州,特别是费城以西。受影响人群包括接触 麻疹病毒 的个人,该州总共记录了 1,004 例病例。

宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 暴发 病例已达到 1,004 例,这是该州三十多年来最大的 麻疹 病例。卫生官员证实了这一点,福克斯 5 亚特兰大频道报道了这一事件。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致 5 人死亡、近 200 人住院。这是更大的全国趋势的一部分,美国各地报告了近 3,900 例病例,是 1991 年以来的最高水平。由于该病毒的传染性和进一步传播的可能性,该风险被明确列为高风险。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania measles outbreak passes 1,000 cases in worst year for US since 1991 - FOX 5 Atlanta

News Brief
A measles outbreak in Pennsylvania has surpassed 1,000 cases, marking the largest outbreak in a state in over three decades. The outbreak began in April 2026 and has led to five deaths and nearly 200 hospitalizations. Health officials attribute the surge to low vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold needed to prevent outbreaks. This outbreak contributes to a national total of nearly 3,900 cases, the highest since 1991. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) highlight the virus's high contagiousness, with up to 9 out of 10 susceptible individuals likely to contract it upon exposure. International health officials are set to meet to reassess the U.S. and Mexico's measles-free status. The outbreak is reported by FOX 5 Atlanta, with data sourced from the Centers for Disease Control and the Associated Press. The situation remains under active investigation, and the response includes monitoring and addressing vaccination rates.

passes 1,000 cases in worst year for US since 1991 By Nick Viviani FOX Local Health Published October 5, 2026 11:05 PM EDT Share Copy Link Email Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Reddit Published October 5, 2026 11:05 PM EDT article Paramyxovirus measles virus, and virions of the polyomavirus, simian virus SV40, revealed in the photomicrograph film, 1981. Image courtesy Centers for Disease Control (CDC) / Dr Erskine Palmer. (Photo by Smith Collection/Gado/Getty Images) The Brief Health officials in Pennsylvania have recorded 1,004 measles cases since an outbreak began in April. Five people have died in connection with the outbreak and nearly 200 hospitalizations have been reported. Nearly 3,900 measles cases have been reported across the United States, the most since 1991. A measles outbreak in Pennsylvania has become the largest one experienced by a state in more than three decades as the number of reported cases crosses into four-digit territory. Local perspective: Pennsylvania health officials have recorded 1,004 cases since April, when the outbreak began west of Philadelphia. They also indicated that nearly 200 hospitalizations have occurred and five deaths have been reported. By the numbers: That number of cases pushes Pennsylvania’s total past other states where large outbreaks have occurred this year, including Texas and South Carolina, where an outbreak resulted in 997 cases. Those major outbreaks have helped drive the overall number of cases in the United States to nearly 3,900, which is the highest total since 1991. The last time a single state recorded an outbreak as large as what is happening in Pennsylvania was in the early 1990s, when 4,500 cases were reported in New York City over the course of multiple years. RELATED: Recall issued for over 122,000 cases of Gatorade sold in 37 states due to undeclared food colorings Big picture view: Health officials are blaming the outbreaks on low vaccination rates. Vaccinations were credited with helping the U.S. eliminate the domestic spread of measles in 2000. Since then, however, the vaccination rate has fallen below the 95% coverage considered necessary to keep outbreaks from happening. Now, international health officials plan to meet next month to decide whether the U.S. and Mexico should still be considered measles-free. Dig deeper: Measles is caused by a highly contagious virus. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention states that up to 9 out of 10 people who are susceptible and exposed to the virus will get it. The virus can spread when people who have it breathe, cough, or sneeze, or it can be transferred on contaminated surfaces. The Source: Information for this article was taken from the Associated Press. This story was reported from Orlando. News Brief
The National Foundation for Infectious Diseases (NFID) reported on a measles outbreak in Pennsylvania on October 6, 2026, emphasizing three key lessons for the United States.
